Structured deformations for energies with general surface terms
Davide Donati and Manuel Friedrich

Abstract
. We develop a variational theory of structured deformations for energies whose surface densities satisfy general growth conditions. This requires a formulation in the generalised space GBV ⋆ {\rm GBV}_{\star} , introduced by Dal Maso and Toader, which is the natural setting for surface energies that are linear near the origin and bounded at infinity. In this framework, we prove three main results: an approximation theorem for structured deformations, an integral representation theorem for abstract lower semicontinuous functionals, and an explicit representation formula for relaxed energies. The proofs rely on new density results for functions of bounded variation and on Poincaré-type inequalities tailored to GBV ⋆ {\rm GBV}_{\star} . Our results extend the applicability of structured deformations to cohesive models in fracture mechanics. MSC codes: 49J45, 49Q20, 74A60, 74M99 Keywords: Structured deformations, Relaxation, Approximation results, Generalised functions of bounded variation, Cohesive energies



1.
Introduction

The purpose of this paper is to establish a theory for the description of first-order structured deformations with general nonlinear surface densities. Structured deformations were introduced by Del Piero & Owen
[
37
]
as a mathematical framework to describe multiscale phenomena in materials. In classical continuum mechanics, the behaviour of materials is typically described by an injective map

g\colon\Omega\to\mathbb{R}^{d}
, defined on a reference configuration
\Omega\subset\mathbb{R}^{d}
, whose gradient
\nabla g
represents the local distortions. In the theory of structured deformations, this description is enriched by replacing
g
by a triple
(\kappa,g,G)
: the map
g
still describes the macroscopic deformation, but it is now allowed to have discontinuities, which are concentrated on the closed set
\kappa
, interpreted as a
crack site
. The matrix field
G
accounts for the contributions at the macroscopical level of smooth sub-macroscopic changes, while the tensor
\nabla g-G
captures those arising from non-smooth sub-macroscopic changes, such as slips and separations (referred to as
disarrangements

[
38
]
).
One of the main contributions of
[
37
]
consists in an
approximation result
that clarifies this interpretation: every structured deformation
(\kappa,g,G)
can be seen as the limit of
simple deformations
, i.e., triples of the form
\{(\kappa_{n},g_{n},\nabla g_{n})\}_{n}
, such that the tensors
G
and
\nabla g-G
arise from the approximating sequences
\{g_{n}\}_{n}
and
\{\nabla g_{n}\}_{n}
in the following way:
G
is the weak limit of
\nabla g_{n}
, while
\nabla g-G
is given by
\nabla(\lim_{n}g_{n})-\lim_{n}\nabla g_{n}
, thus representing a measure of noncommutability of the operations
\nabla
and
\lim_{n}
. The theory of structured deformations can model a broad class of mechanical phenomena in which the macroscopic response is influenced by microscopic slips, cracks, or other disarrangements, including elasticity, plasticity, fracture, and the mechanics of defective crystalline solids.

Adopting this point of view, Choksi & Fonseca
[
25
]
laid the foundations for a variational theory of structured deformations. In their formulation, the triple
(\kappa,g,G)
is replaced by a pair
(g,G)
, where the deformation map
g
is a function in
{\rm SBV}(\Omega;\mathbb{R}^{k})
and
G\in L^{p}(\Omega;\mathbb{R}^{k\times d})
for some
p\geq 1
. The crack site
\kappa
is no longer prescribed independently,
but is instead represented by the jump set
J_{g}
of the deformation map. Relying on a previous result of Alberti
[
1
]
, they were able to recover an analogue of the approximation theorem of Del Piero & Owen: every pair
(g,G)
can be approximated, in a suitable sense, by sequences
\{(u_{n},\nabla u_{n})\}_{n}
with
\{u_{n}\}_{n}\subset{\rm SBV}(\Omega;\mathbb{R}^{k})
,
\nabla u_{n}
being the approximate gradient of
u_{n}
.
Departing from a general class of energy functionals with bulk and surface densities

(1.1)

\displaystyle\mathcal{E}(u)=\int_{\Omega}\Psi(x,\nabla u)\,{\rm d}x+\int_{J_{u}\cap\Omega}\gamma(x,[u],\nu_{u})\,{\rm d}\mathcal{H}^{d-1},

this result justified the assignment of a
relaxed formulation

I^{p}
representing the energetically most favourable way to approximate a structured deformation, namely

\displaystyle I^{p}(g,G)\coloneq\inf\Big\{

\displaystyle\liminf_{n\to+\infty}\mathcal{E}(u_{n})\colon\,\{u_{n}\}_{n}\subset{\rm SBV}(\Omega;\mathbb{R}^{k})\text{ and }\{(u_{n},\nabla u_{n})\}_{n}\text{ converges to }(g,G)\Big\}.

Here,
[u]\coloneq u^{+}-u^{-}
is the difference of the unilateral traces
u^{+},u^{-}
of
u
on
J_{u}
, whose unit normal is given by
\nu_{u}
,
\Psi
is a non-negative integrand with
p
-growth, for
p\geq 1
, and
\gamma
satisfies

(1.2)

c|\zeta|\leq\gamma(x,\zeta,\nu)\leq C|\zeta|

for suitable positive constants
c
and
C
.
Under these assumptions, Choksi & Fonseca identified effective bulk and surface
energy densities which are defined through cell formulas (computed by looking at simple deformations
(u,\nabla u)
only) and which allow to represent
I^{p}
as an integral functional.

This variational approach has been developed further over the last years, including higher-order and multi-level theories

[
40
,
14
,
15
,
16
,
9
,
13
]
, linearised models
[
42
]
, as well as dimension reduction
[
47
,
22
]
and homogenisation
[
3
]
. For a comprehensive introduction to the theory and for an exhaustive list of contributions on the topic,
we refer the interested reader to the recent monograph

[
45
]
.

While being satisfactory for an array of applications, the growth condition (
1.2
) excludes, however, a large plethora of relevant settings, such as models for defects in crystals or cohesive zone models in fracture mechanics. There, it is natural to assume that for small jump openings the function
\zeta\mapsto\gamma(x,\zeta,\nu)

exhibits a linear behaviour, while it saturates for large jump openings, approaching a positive constant as
|\zeta|\to+\infty
.
To treat such applications in the framework of structured deformations, it is thus necessary to relax the bounds in
(
1.2
) and to consider surface densities satisfying

(1.3)

c(\color[rgb]{0,0,0}|\zeta|\land 1)\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\leq\gamma(x,\zeta,\nu)\leq C(\color[rgb]{0,0,0}|\zeta|\land 1)\color[rgb]{0,0,0},

where
a\land b\coloneq\min\{a,b\}
for
a,b\in\mathbb{R}
. The main scope of this paper is to systematically study the relaxation of energies of the form (
1
) under the growth assumption (
1.3
).

Some observations in this direction have already been made, see, e.g., the discussions in
[
25
, Remark 3.3]
or
[
46
, Remark 3.1]
. However, the suggested approach in
[
25
,
46
]
comes at the expense of altering the definition of the relaxation
I^{p}
by considering only approximating sequences with
equibounded
{\rm BV}
-norm
. As the lower bound in (
1.3
) does not guarantee this equiboundedness property, this may lead to an ill-posed existence theory for minimisation problems associated to the relaxed energy
I^{p}
. A natural way to circumvent this limitation is to generalise the admissible class of deformation fields
g

to the natural domain of energies with surface densities as in (
1.3
). This corresponds to considering the space of generalised functions of bounded variation

{\rm GBV}_{\star}(\Omega;\mathbb{R}^{k})
, introduced in the scalar case by Dal Maso & Toader

[
32
]
and extended to the vector-valued setting in

[
39
]
. In fact, this space has been tailored to study problems in cohesive fracture and fractured elastoplastic materials featuring models with surface densities of the form (
1.3
), and has already been employed successfully in dealing with several variational problems, see
[
32
,
39
,
33
,
34
,
30
]
. In particular, in this setting suitable compactness results are available, see Remark
3.17
.

Our goal is to provide a rather complete picture of the relaxation of
I^{p}
in the class of admissible deformation fields
g\in{\rm GBV}_{\star}(\Omega;\mathbb{R}^{k})
, under the growth assumption (
1.3
), without requiring any additional constraint on the norms of the approximating sequences.
Our results can be summarised as follows:

(a)

an extension of the approximation theorem of Choksi & Fonseca, where we prove that every pair
(g,G)
, with
g\in{\rm GBV}_{\star}(\Omega;\mathbb{R}^{k})
and
G\in L^{1}(\Omega;\mathbb{R}^{k\times d})
, can be
approximated by means of a sequence
\{u_{n}\}_{n}\subset{\rm SBV}(\Omega;\mathbb{R}^{k})
(see Theorem
3.1
);

(b)

an integral representation result for abstract functionals by means of the global method for relaxation of Bouchitté, Fonseca, & Mascarenhas
[
18
]
(see Theorem
3.7
);

(c)

an explicit integral representation for the relaxation of (
1
), under the growth condition (
1.3
) (see Theorems
3.13
,
3.14
, and
3.16
).

Besides the technical difficulties inherent to the function space, the approach exhibits two major conceptual difficulties that render the analysis substantially different from the existing literature on structured deformations on the one hand, and from models formulated on
{\rm GBV}_{\star}
on the other hand:

(i)

The first is related to the approximation theorem: in contrast to the result in
[
25
]
, in our setting it is not meaningful to ask that the total variation
|Du_{n}|(\Omega)
of the approximating sequence is controlled by
|Dg|(\Omega)+\|G\|_{L^{1}(\Omega)}
, but the total variation on both sides must be replaced by the nonlinear quantity
\mathcal{V}
defined by

\mathcal{V}(g,O)\coloneq\sum_{i=1}^{k}\Big(\int_{O}|\nabla g_{i}|\,{\rm d}x+|D^{c}g_{i}|(O)+\int_{J_{g_{i}}\cap O}|[g_{i}]|\wedge 1\,{\rm d}\mathcal{H}^{d-1}\Big)\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\quad\text{for $O\subset\Omega$}.\color[rgb]{0,0,0}

Here,
\nabla g_{i}
corresponds to the approximate gradient,
D^{c}g_{i}
to the Cantor part, and
J_{g_{i}}
to the jump set of the
i
-th component of
g
. Although the space
{\rm GBV}_{\star}
is far more general than
{\rm BV}
, it retains the fine structure needed to study free discontinuity problems, at the expense of replacing the control of the total variation
|Dg|
by the weaker, energetically natural quantity
\mathcal{V}
.

(ii)

In the abstract integral representation result based on the global method, we cannot resort to the by-now classical approach in
{\rm(G)SBV}
and
{\rm GBV}_{\star}
, which relies on a
perturbation-truncation
argument, see e.g.
[
7
,
8
,
10
,
11
,
19
,
33
,
30
,
34
]
. More precisely, the usual approach consists in perturbing a functional satisfying (
1.3
) by a surface term, leading to a growth condition of the form (
1
), which allows to employ the integral representation results in
[
17
]
or in
[
18
]
. Afterwards, the perturbation parameter is sent to zero by means of a suitable truncation technique. While this strategy has been successfully employed in
{\rm GBV}_{\star}
in
[
33
,
34
,
30
]
, the presence of the matrix field
G
prevents us from carrying out the same analysis. This requires careful modifications and local truncations in blow-up constructions around bulk and surface points, thereby avoiding any perturbation argument.

We further comment on the three main results (a)–(c) highlighting ingredients in the proof.

(a)
The requirement of a uniform control on
\mathcal{V}
along the approximating sequence, which is an
essential feature of the Approximation Theorem
3.1
, makes the proof of this result highly non-trivial. In particular, it requires a novel density result for functions of bounded variation. More precisely, it relies crucially on Theorem
4.1
(see also Remark
4.2
(i),(iii)), which shows that every function
u\in{\rm GBV}_{\star}(\Omega;\mathbb{R}^{k})
can be approximated
by a sequence
\{u_{n}\}_{n}\subset{\rm SBV}(\Omega;\mathbb{R}^{k})\cap L^{\infty}(\Omega;\mathbb{R}^{k})
in such a way that
\mathcal{V}(u_{n},\Omega\setminus J_{u_{n}})
and
\mathcal{V}(u_{n},J_{u_{n}})
converge to
\mathcal{V}(u,\Omega\setminus J_{u})
and to
\mathcal{V}(u,J_{u})
, respectively. This is achieved by exploiting a classical result of Goffman & Serrin
[
43
, Theorem 4
′
]
to approximate the function
u
far from the jump set
J_{u}
, while a hands-on construction is performed close to
J_{u}
.
This result can also be adapted to more general energies with linear growth (see Remark
4.2
(ii)) and, combining it with the recent approximation result
[
27
, Theorem 1.1]
for functions with possibly infinite jump set, it shows that
{\rm GBV}_{\star}
-functions can be approximated in energy by piecewise affine functions with piecewise affine jump set. We hope that this result might be of independent interest and that our proposed strategy may find applications beyond the setting of the present work.

(b) In the second part of the paper, we consider
a class
\mathfrak{F}^{p}_{\star}
of relevant local functionals satisfying two-sided growth conditions in terms of
\mathcal{V}(g,\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\Omega)\color[rgb]{0,0,0}+\|G\|_{L^{p}}^{p}
, see Definition
3.3
. We then employ the global method

[
18
,
17
]
to characterise its
absolutely continuous and its jump part as integral functionals. As customary in this approach, the
corresponding bulk and surface densities are obtained through asymptotic
cell formulas on small cubes, with affine boundary data for the bulk part
and pure jump boundary data for the surface part (see (
3.5
) and (
3.6
)). Both formulas also take into account constraints on the average of the matrix fields
G
.

We stress that in the setting of structured deformations we have to face some serious obstacles in implementing this well-known strategy. In particular, as mentioned already before, we are forced to adopt an approach deviating from the one which is frequently employed for generalised functions of bounded variation.
This is mainly due to the fact that the relaxation of (
1
) does not satisfy the ‘truncation properties’ that would allow us to implement the abovementioned perturbation-truncation method. To solve this issue, we follow an approach more similar to the one employed in integral representation results in
{\rm SBV}^{p}

[
17
]
and in
{\rm(G)SBD}^{p}

[
26
,
28
]
, which is based on a careful analysis of the blow-ups of such functions (see
[
17
, Lemmas 2 and 3]
,
[
26
, Lemma 3.4]
, and
[
28
, Lemmas 5.1 and 6.1]
). In these works, such analysis is made possible by the use of Poincaré and Korn-Poincaré inequalities that allow for a better control on the quantities at play. Following this reasoning, in Theorem
C.3
we provide an adaptation to
{\rm GBV}_{\star}(\Omega;\mathbb{R}^{k})
of the Poincaré inequality of De Giorgi, Carriero, & Leaci
[
36
]
, which we then use in Lemmas
D.1
and
D.2
to perform the required blow-up analysis. With these tools at our disposal, we are then able to proceed with the global method.

(c) In the final part of the paper, we provide an integral representation for the relaxation of (
1
) under the growth condition (
1.3
). As an application of Theorem
3.7
, we first prove in Theorem
3.13
that the bulk and surface parts can always be represented as integral functionals, whose densities we denote by
f_{\rm bulk}
and
f_{\rm surf}
. We then specialise to the case
p>1
, corresponding to superlinear growth of the bulk density
\Psi
. Here, some truncation properties are available (see Lemmas
6.2
and
6.5
), and we are able to prove (see Theorem
3.14
) that
f_{\rm bulk}
and
f_{\rm surf}
are given by cell formulas consistent with the ones identified by Choksi & Fonseca (see (
3.22
) and (
3.23
) and compare with
[
25
, Remark 3.3]
). Finally, in the case that
\Psi
and
\gamma
are independent of the spatial variable
x
, we obtain a complete representation of the
relaxed functional, including its Cantor part, which is expressed in
terms of the recession function of the bulk energy density. This is done relying on the relaxation result
[
18
, Theorem 3.12]
, similarly to what was done, for instance, for measure-valued structured deformations
[
44
, Theorem 2.4]
and for structured deformations in linearised elasticity
[
42
, Theorem 2.12]
.

We briefly describe the organisation of the paper. In Section
2
we fix
the notation and recall the definition and the main properties of the space

{\rm GBV}_{\star}
. In Section
3
we set the notation concerning
generalised
structured deformations
{\rm SD}^{p}_{\star}
and present the statements of the main results. Section
4
is devoted to the proof of the approximation
theorem and to the auxiliary density results on which it relies. In
Section
5
we prove the abstract integral representation theorem for
functionals in
\mathfrak{F}^{p}_{\star}
. In Section
6
we apply the results of Section
5
to analyse the properties of the relaxed energy. The appendices collect several new technical
tools concerning functions in
{\rm GBV}_{\star}(\Omega;\mathbb{R}^{k})
. In Appendix
A
we give a brief introduction to the fine properties of generalised functions of bounded variation. This is complemented by the analysis carried out in Appendix
B
, where we study their slicing properties. Appendix
C
is devoted to the proof of Poincaré-type inequalities, which are then used to obtain the blow-up lemmas of Appendix
D
. Finally, in Appendix
E
we present a well-known result concerning lower semicontinuous integral functionals with linear growth.

2.
Preliminaries on generalised functions of bounded variation

In this section we fix notation and present some important definitions.

Notation:
Throughout the paper,
d,k\in\mathbb{N}
, and
\Omega\subset\mathbb{R}^{d}
is always a bounded open subset of
\mathbb{R}^{d}
with Lipschitz boundary.
For
m\in\mathbb{N}
the scalar product between two vectors
a,b\in\mathbb{R}^{m}
is denoted by
a\cdot b
, while the Euclidean norm of
\mathbb{R}^{m}
is denoted by
|\,\cdot\,|
. For every
\rho>0
and
x\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
, the open ball with centre
x
and radius
\rho
is denoted by
B_{\rho}(x)
; we omit the indication of the centre
x
if
x=0.
The unit sphere of
\mathbb{R}^{d}
is denoted by

\mathbb{S}^{d-1}\coloneq\{\nu\in\mathbb{R}^{d}:|\nu|=1\}
. Given
x\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
,
\nu\in\mathbb{S}^{d-1}
, and
\rho>0
, we let
Q_{\nu}(x,\rho)
be a cube centred at
x
, of side-length
\rho
, and with two faces orthogonal to
\nu
. When
\nu=e_{d}
, we omit the dependence on
\nu
and assume that
Q(x,\rho)\coloneq x+(-\rho/2,\rho/2)^{d}.
We also simply write
Q
if
x=0
and
\rho=1
.

We identify the vector space
\mathbb{R}^{k\times d}
with the space of
k\times d
matrices, endowed with the Frobenius norm. Given
A\in\mathbb{R}^{k\times d}
, its
ij
-th component is denoted by
A_{ij}
.
The tensor product
a\otimes b\in\mathbb{R}^{k\times d}
between two vectors
a\in\mathbb{R}^{k}
and
b\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
is the matrix whose
ij
-th component is given by
(a\otimes b)_{ij}\coloneq a_{i}b_{j}
.

For
m\in\mathbb{N}
, the symbol
\mathcal{M}_{b}(\Omega;\mathbb{R}^{m})
denotes the space of all bounded
\mathbb{R}^{m}
-valued Radon measures. We omit the indication of the target space if
m=1
. The space of all positive Radon measures is denoted by
\mathcal{M}^{+}(\Omega)
. The subspace of
\mathcal{M}^{+}(\Omega)
of all bounded positive measures is denoted by
\mathcal{M}^{+}_{b}(\Omega)
. Given
\mu\in\mathcal{M}_{b}(\Omega;\mathbb{R}^{m}\color[rgb]{0,0,0})
and
\lambda\in\mathcal{M}^{+}_{b}(\Omega)
,
{\rm d}\mu/{\rm d}\lambda
is the Radon-Nikodým derivative of
\mu
with respect to
\lambda
, while
|\mu|
denotes the total variation measure of
\mu
.

Let
\mathcal{L}^{m}
and
\mathcal{H}^{m}
denote the
m
-dimensional Lebesgue measure and the
m
-dimensional Hausdorff measure, respectively. Given a Borel measure
\mu
on
\Omega
and a
\mu
-measurable set
E\subset\Omega
, we let
\mu\mathbin{\vrule height=6.88889pt,depth=0.0pt,width=0.55974pt\vrule height=0.55974pt,depth=0.0pt,width=5.59721pt}E
be the restricted measure defined by
(\mu\mathbin{\vrule height=6.88889pt,depth=0.0pt,width=0.55974pt\vrule height=0.55974pt,depth=0.0pt,width=5.59721pt}E)(B)\coloneq\mu(E\cap B)
for every Borel set
B\subset\Omega
. Given an
\mathcal{L}^{d}
-measurable subset
E\subset\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathbb{R}^{d}
, we denote by
L^{0}(E;\mathbb{R}^{m})
the collection of all
\mathcal{L}^{d}
-measurable functions
u\colon E\to\mathbb{R}^{m}
. We endow the space
L^{0}(E;\mathbb{R}^{m})
with the topology associated with the convergence in measure, which we recall to be separable and metrisable. In particular, a distance inducing this topology is given by

d_{E}(u,v)\coloneq\int_{E}|u-v|\land 1\,{\rm d}x,

where
a\land b\coloneq\min\{a,b\}
for any
a,b\in\mathbb{R}
.

Given an
\mathcal{L}^{d}
-measurable set
E\subset\mathbb{R}^{d}
, we denote by
\chi_{E}
its characteristic function, defined by
\chi_{E}(x)=1
if
x\in E
and
\chi_{E}(x)=0
otherwise. If
E
is of finite perimeter, we denote by
\partial^{*}E
its reduced boundary. Moreover, given
x\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
and
\rho>0
, we set

(2.1)

\Omega_{\rho,x}\coloneq x+\rho(\Omega-x).

The collection of all open (resp. Borel) subsets of
\Omega
is denoted by
\mathcal{O}(\Omega)
(resp.
\mathcal{B}(\Omega
)). Given two open sets
O^{\prime},O^{\prime\prime}\subset\mathbb{R}^{d}
we write
O^{\prime}\subset\subset O^{\prime\prime}
if
\overline{O^{\prime}}\subset O^{\prime\prime}
.

(Generalised) functions of bounded variation.
We recall the definition and the main properties of the function spaces used in this work. In particular, we present the spaces
{\rm GBV}(\Omega;\mathbb{R}^{k})
and
{\rm GBV}_{\star}(\Omega;\mathbb{R}^{k})
, the first introduced by De Giorgi & Ambrosio
[
35
]
and later investigated in
[
4
]
, the latter by Dal Maso & Toader
[
32
]
in the scalar setting and later generalised to the vector-valued case in
[
39
]
. We give only the basic definitions here and refer the reader to Appendix
A
for further properties, including the definition of approximate gradient and jump set of
\mathcal{L}^{d}
-measurable functions.

The space
{\rm BV}(\Omega;\mathbb{R}^{k})
is the space of all functions
u\in L^{1}(\Omega;\mathbb{R}^{k})
whose distributional derivative
Du
belongs to
\mathcal{M}_{b}(\Omega;\mathbb{R}^{k\times d})
. We recall that the measure
Du
can be decomposed as

Du=\nabla u\mathcal{L}^{d}+D^{c}u+([u]\otimes\nu_{u})\mathcal{H}^{d-1}\mathbin{\vrule height=6.88889pt,depth=0.0pt,width=0.55974pt\vrule height=0.55974pt,depth=0.0pt,width=5.59721pt}J_{u},

where

•

\nabla u\in L^{1}(\Omega;\mathbb{R}^{k\times d})
is the
approximate gradient
of
u
(see (
A.3
));

•

the
Cantor part

D^{c}u
is a measure that is singular with respect to
\mathcal{L}^{d}
and that vanishes on all Borel sets
B\in\mathcal{B}(\Omega)
that are
\sigma
-finite with respect to
\mathcal{H}^{d-1}
;

•

[u]\coloneq u^{+}-u^{-}
is the difference of the two unilateral traces of
u
on the jump set
J_{u}
, whose unit normal is
\nu_{u}
(see (
A.4
)).

The space
{\rm GBV}(\Omega;\mathbb{R}^{k})
is the space of all functions
u\in L^{0}(\Omega;\mathbb{R}^{k})
such that
\Phi\circ u\in{\rm BV}_{\rm loc}(\Omega;\mathbb{R}^{k})
for every Lipschitz function
\Phi\colon\mathbb{R}^{k}\to\mathbb{R}^{k}
whose gradient
\nabla\Phi
has compact support. We recall that
{\rm GBV}(\Omega;\mathbb{R}^{k})
is not a vector space and that, in particular,
{\rm GBV}(\Omega;\mathbb{R}^{k})\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\supsetneq\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\rm GBV}(\Omega)^{k}
if
k>1
(see
[
5
, Example 4.27]
).

It is well-known that every function
u\in{\rm GBV}(\Omega;\mathbb{R}^{k})
admits an approximate gradient
\nabla u
(see (b) of Proposition
A.1
).
It can also be shown (see (d) of Proposition
A.1
) that there is a positive (possibly unbounded) measure
|D^{c}u|
, which generalises the total variation of the Cantor part of the distributional gradient of a function of bounded variation.

We now recall the definition of the space
{\rm GBV}_{\star}(\Omega;\mathbb{R}^{k})
.
To this end, we introduce the functional
\mathcal{V}\colon L^{0}(\Omega;\mathbb{R}^{k})\times\mathcal{B}(\Omega)\to[0,+\infty]
which for every
O\in\mathcal{O}(\Omega)
is defined by

(2.2)

\mathcal{V}(u,O)\coloneq\begin{cases}\displaystyle\sum_{i=1}^{k}\Big(\int_{O}|\nabla u_{i}|\,{\rm d}x+|D^{c}u_{i}|(O)+\int_{J_{u_{i}}\cap O}|[u_{i}]|\land 1\,{\rm d}\mathcal{H}^{d-1}\Big)&\text{ if $u\in{\rm GBV}(O)^{k}$,}\\
+\infty&\text{ otherwise}.\end{cases}

Here,
u_{i}
denotes the
i
-th component of
u
.
The definition is then extended to every Borel set
B\in\mathcal{B}(\Omega)
by setting

(2.3)

\mathcal{V}(u,B)\coloneq\inf\{\mathcal{V}(u,O)\colon\,O\supset B,\,O\in\mathcal{O}(\Omega)\}.

Remark 2.1
.

It follows from
[
32
, Theorem 6.1]
that for every
O\in\mathcal{O}(\Omega)
the functional
\mathcal{V}(\cdot,O)
is lower semicontinuous with respect to the
L^{0}(\Omega;\mathbb{R}^{k})
-convergence.

For
O\in\mathcal{O}(\Omega)
, the space
{\rm GBV}_{\star}(O;\mathbb{R}^{k})
is defined as

(2.4)

{\rm GBV}_{\star}(O;\mathbb{R}^{k})\coloneq\big\{u\in{\rm GBV}(O)^{k}\colon\,\mathcal{V}(u,O)<+\infty\big\},

or, equivalently, as

\displaystyle{\rm GBV}_{\star}(O;\mathbb{R}^{k})\coloneq\{u\in{\rm GBV}(O)^{k}

\displaystyle\colon\,\nabla u\in L^{1}(O;\mathbb{R}^{k\times d}),|D^{c}u|\in\mathcal{M}_{b}(O),

\displaystyle\ |[u]|\in L^{1}_{\mathcal{H}^{d-1}}(J_{u}\setminus J^{1}_{u}),\text{ and }\mathcal{H}^{d-1}(J^{1}_{u})<+\infty\},

where, for
r\geq 0
, we set

(2.5)

J^{r}_{u}\coloneq\{x\in J_{u}\colon\,|[u]|\geq r\}.

It was shown in
[
32
, Theorem 3.9]
and
[
39
, Proposition 3.3]
that
{\rm GBV}_{\star}(O;\mathbb{R}^{k})
is a vector space. Moreover, it follows from definition (
2.4
) that for every
u\in{\rm GBV}_{\star}(O;\mathbb{R}^{k})
and
i\in\{1,\dots,k\}
the
i
-th component
u_{i}
belongs to
{\rm GBV}_{\star}(O)
.

To each
u\in{\rm GBV}_{\star}(O;\mathbb{R}^{k})
, one can associate a
matrix-valued
measure
D^{c}u\in\mathcal{M}_{b}(O\color[rgb]{0,0,0};\mathbb{R}^{k\times d})
that generalises the Cantor part of the distributional derivative for functions of bounded variation. We remark that the total variation of
D^{c}u
coincides with the measure
|D^{c}u|
introduced before for functions in
{\rm GBV}
(see (d) of Proposition
A.1
). We refer the reader to Proposition
A.5
for the precise definition of this measure.

3.
Setting of the problem and main results

In this section, we present the setting and the main results of the paper. We begin by introducing the class of structured deformations that will be our main object of investigation.

Structured Deformations.
Throughout the work, let
p\geq 1
. We specify only when needed if
p=1
or
p>1
. We recall that
\Omega\subset\mathbb{R}^{d}
always denotes a bounded, open set with Lipschitz boundary. Given
O\in\mathcal{O}(\Omega)\color[rgb]{0,0,0}
, the class of
generalised structured deformations

{\rm SD}^{p}_{\star}(O;\mathbb{R}^{k})
is defined by setting

{\rm SD}_{\star}^{p}(O;\mathbb{R}^{k})\coloneq\big\{(g,G)\colon\,g\in{\rm GBV}_{\star}(O;\mathbb{R}^{k})\,\text{ and }G\in L^{p}(O;\mathbb{R}^{k\times d})\big\}.

We use the term ‘generalised’ as the deformations
g
belong to a class of generalised
{\rm BV}
-functions.
The first main result of this work is the following approximation result for structured deformations
{\rm SD}_{\star}^{p}(\Omega;\mathbb{R}^{k})
. Recall the definition of
\mathcal{V}

in (
2.3
).

Theorem 3.1

(Approximation Theorem)
.

There exists a constant
C=C(d)>0
such that for all
O\in\mathcal{O}(\Omega)
with Lipschitz boundary and all
(g,G)\in{\rm SD}^{p}_{\star}(O;\mathbb{R}^{k})
we can find a sequence
\{u_{n}\}_{n}\subset{\rm SBV}(O;\mathbb{R}^{k})
such that
\{u_{n}\}_{n}
converges to
g
in
L^{0}(O;\mathbb{R}^{k})
as
n\to+\infty
,
\nabla u_{n}=G

\mathcal{L}^{d}
-a.e. in
O
for all
n\in\mathbb{N}
, and

(3.1)

\limsup_{n\to+\infty}\mathcal{V}(u_{n},O)\leq C\Big(\mathcal{V}(g,O)+\|G\|_{L^{1}(O)}\Big).

We briefly comment on this result. If (
3.1
) is dropped, the theorem is an immediate consequence of
[
49
, Theorem 1.2]
(see also
[
25
, Theorem 2.12]
). Indeed, using the truncation properties of
{\rm GBV}_{\star}
-functions described in Proposition
A.3
, we can approximate
g
by means of
{\rm BV}
-functions, to which
[
49
, Theorem 1.2]
applies. A diagonal argument then gives a sequence converging to
g
in
L^{0}(O;\mathbb{R}^{k})
which satisfies the required gradient constraints, but which may fail to satisfy (
3.1
). To overcome this difficulty, we will depart from the results currently available in the literature, and rely on new density results for functions of bounded variation tailored to our functional setting, namely Theorem
4.1
and Lemma
4.3
. In particular, Theorem
4.1
provides an approximation result for
{\rm BV}
-functions by means of
{\rm SBV}
-functions that applies to a general class of energies, including
\mathcal{V}
(see Remark
4.2
(ii)). We hope that this result, which is of independent interest, may find applications also in other contexts, for instance, when addressing the
\Gamma
-limsup inequality in the asymptotic analysis of free discontinuity problems.

We endow
{\rm SD}^{p}_{\star}(\Omega;\mathbb{R}^{k})
with the following notion of convergence.

Definition 3.2
.

We say that a sequence
\{(g_{n},G_{n})\}_{n}\subset{\rm SD}^{p}_{\star}(\Omega;\mathbb{R}^{k})
converges to
(g,G)\in{\rm SD}^{p}_{\star}(\Omega;\mathbb{R}^{k})
as
n\to+\infty
, in symbols
(g_{n},G_{n})\mathbin{{\mathop{\to}\limits}}(g,G)
in
\Omega
, if and only if
g_{n}\to g
in
L^{0}(\Omega;\mathbb{R}^{k})
and

G_{n}\rightharpoonup G\text{ in $L^{p}(\Omega;\mathbb{R}^{k\times d})$ if $p>1$}\quad\text{ and }\quad G_{n}\rightharpoonup^{*}G\text{ weakly${}^{*}$ in $\mathcal{M}_{b}(\Omega;\mathbb{R}^{k\times d})$ if $p=1$}

as
n\to+\infty.

Given a sequence
\{u_{n}\}_{n}\subset{\rm SBV}(\Omega;\mathbb{R}^{k})
, we say that
\{u_{n}\}_{n}
converges to
(g,G)\in{\rm SD}^{p}_{\star}(\Omega;\mathbb{R}^{k})
, in symbols
u_{n}\mathbin{{\mathop{\to}\limits}}(g,G)
, if
(u_{n},\nabla u_{n})\mathbin{{\mathop{\to}\limits}}(g,G)
.

Integral representation for abstract functionals.

We present a result concerning the integral representation of functionals defined on
{\rm SD}^{p}_{\star}(\Omega;\mathbb{R}^{k})
. We begin by introducing the class
\mathfrak{F}^{p}_{\star}
, which will be one of the main objects of study in this paper.

Definition 3.3
.

\mathfrak{F}^{p}_{\star}
is the class of all functionals
\mathcal{F}\colon{\rm SD}^{p}_{\star}(\Omega;\mathbb{R}^{k})\times\mathcal{B}(\Omega)\to[0,+\infty)
satisfying the following conditions:

(
{\rm H}
1)

(
Measure property
)
for every
(g,G)\in{\rm SD}^{p}_{\star}(\Omega;\mathbb{R}^{k})
the set function
\mathcal{F}(g,G,\cdot)
is a Radon measure on
\mathcal{B}(\Omega)
;

(
{\rm H}
2)

(
Locality
)
for every
O\in\mathcal{O}(\Omega)
and pairs
(g,G),(u,U)\in{\rm SD}^{p}_{\star}(\Omega;\mathbb{R}^{k})
, with
g=u
and
G=U

\mathcal{L}^{d}
-a.e. in
O
, it holds
\mathcal{F}(g,G,O)=\mathcal{F}(u,U,O)
;

(
{\rm H}
3)

(
Lower semicontinuity
)
for every
O\in\mathcal{O}(\Omega)
the functional
\mathcal{F}(\cdot,\cdot,O)
is lower semicontinuous with respect to the convergence in
{\rm SD}^{p}_{\star}(\Omega;\mathbb{R}^{k})
;

(
{\rm H}
4)

(
Growth conditions
)
there exist positive constants
\alpha,\beta
, with
\alpha\leq\beta
, such that

(3.2)

\alpha\Big(\mathcal{V}(g,B)+\|G\|^{p}_{L^{p}(B)}\Big)\leq\mathcal{F}(g,G,B)\leq\beta\Big(\mathcal{V}(g,B)+\|G\|^{p}_{L^{p}(B)}+\mathcal{L}^{d}(B)\Big)

for every
(g,G)\in{\rm SD}^{p}_{\star}(\Omega;\mathbb{R}^{k})
and
B\in\mathcal{B}(\Omega)
.

Remark 3.4
.

Combining properties
(

H

1)
and
(

H

2)
we obtain that every functional
\mathcal{F}\in\mathfrak{F}^{p}_{\star}
satisfies the following locality property: if
B\in\mathcal{B}(\Omega)
,
(g,G),(u,U)\in{\rm SD}^{p}_{\star}(\Omega;\mathbb{R}^{k})
, and
g=u
and
G=U

\mathcal{L}^{d}
-a.e. in a neighbourhood of
B
, then
\mathcal{F}(g,G,B)=\mathcal{F}(u,U,B)
.

For this reason, given
O\in\mathcal{O}(\Omega)
with Lipschitz boundary and
(g,G)\in{\rm SD}^{p}_{\star}(O;\mathbb{R}^{k})
, for every
B\in\mathcal{B}(O)
we can define
\mathcal{F}(g,G,B)\coloneq\mathcal{F}(u,U,B)
, where
(u,U)\in{\rm SD}^{p}_{\star}(\Omega;\mathbb{R}^{k})
is any pair such that
g=u
and
G=U

\mathcal{L}^{d}
-a.e. on
O
. For instance, this extension can be performed by using Lemma
A.7
. The locality property described above implies that the value of
\mathcal{F}(g,G,B)
does not depend on the chosen extension
(u,U)
.

In Section
5
we prove an integral representation result for functionals in
\mathfrak{F}^{p}_{\star}
. To give the precise statement of this result, we introduce a decomposition of elements in
\mathfrak{F}^{p}_{\star}
that mimics the usual decomposition of the distributional derivative for functions of bounded variation.

Definition 3.5
.

Let
\mathcal{F}\in\mathfrak{F}^{p}_{\star}
and
(g,G)\in{\rm SD}^{p}_{\star}(\Omega;\mathbb{R}^{k})
. By
(

H

1)
and
(

H

4)
,
\mathcal{F}(g,G,\cdot)
is a bounded Radon measure on
\mathcal{B}(\Omega)
. Its absolutely continuous and singular parts with respect to
\mathcal{L}^{d}
are denoted by
\mathcal{F}^{a}(g,G,\cdot)
and
\mathcal{F}^{s}(g,G,\cdot)
, respectively. We also introduce the bounded Radon measures
\mathcal{F}^{c}(g,G,\cdot)
and
\mathcal{F}^{j}(g,G,\cdot)
defined by

\displaystyle\mathcal{F}^{c}(g,G,B)\coloneq\mathcal{F}^{s}(g,G,B\setminus J_{g})\text{ and }\mathcal{F}^{j}(g,G,B)\coloneq\mathcal{F}(g,G,B\cap J_{g})

for every
B\in\mathcal{B}(\Omega)
.
We obtain

\mathcal{F}(g,G,B)=\mathcal{F}^{a}(g,G,B)+\mathcal{F}^{c}(g,G,B)+\mathcal{F}^{j}(g,G,B)

for every
B\in\mathcal{B}(\Omega)
.

Remark 3.6
.

Note that by Remark
2.1
the functional
(g,G,B)\mapsto\mathcal{V}(g,B)+\|G\|^{p}_{L^{p}(B)}
belongs to
\mathfrak{F}^{p}_{\star}
. Moreover, if
\mathcal{F}\in\mathfrak{F}^{p}_{\star}
and
(g,G)\in{\rm SD}^{p}_{\star}(\Omega;\mathbb{R}^{k})
, then

\mathcal{F}^{a}(g,G,\cdot)
is absolutely continuous with respect to
\mathcal{L}^{d}
,

\mathcal{F}^{c}(g,G,\cdot)
is absolutely continuous with respect to
|D^{c}g|
,

\mathcal{F}^{j}(g,G,\cdot)
is absolutely continuous with respect to
\mathcal{V}(g,\cdot)\mathbin{\vrule height=6.88889pt,depth=0.0pt,width=0.55974pt\vrule height=0.55974pt,depth=0.0pt,width=5.59721pt}J_{g}
.

Our goal is to represent
\mathcal{F}^{a}
and
\mathcal{F}^{j}
as integral functionals. As customary in integral representation results for free discontinuity functionals, the bulk and surface integrands are obtained by considering the asymptotic behaviour of the minimum values of certain constrained problems on small cubes. To this end, given
(g,G)\in{\rm SD}^{p}_{\star}(\Omega;\mathbb{R}^{k})
and
O\in\mathcal{O}(\Omega)
with Lipschitz boundary, we set

\mathcal{C}^{p}_{\star}(g,G,O)\coloneq\Big\{(u,U)\in{\rm SD}^{p}_{\star}(\Omega;\mathbb{R}^{k})\colon\,u=g\,\text{ near }\partial O,\,\int_{O}(G-U)\,{\rm d}x=0\Big\}

and consider the auxiliary minimisation problem given by

(3.3)

\displaystyle\mathfrak{m}(g,G,O)\coloneq\inf\Big\{\mathcal{F}(u,U,O)\colon\,(u,U)\in\mathcal{C}^{p}_{\star}(g,G,O)\Big\}.

The boundary conditions that we generally use for these minimisation problems are given by affine functions and pure jump functions: for
x\in\Omega
,
a\in\mathbb{R}^{k}
, and
A\in\mathbb{R}^{k\times d}
we set
\ell_{x,a,A}(y)\coloneq a+A(y-x)
, and for
x\in\Omega
,
\zeta,\theta\in\mathbb{R}^{k}
,
\nu\in\mathbb{S}^{d-1}
we let

(3.4)

\displaystyle v_{x,\zeta,\theta,\nu}(y)\coloneq\begin{cases}\zeta&\hbox{if }(y-x)\cdot\nu>0,\\
\theta&\hbox{if }(y-x)\cdot\nu\leq 0.\end{cases}

Our candidate bulk and surface integrands are given by

(3.5)

\displaystyle f_{\rm bulk}(x,a,A,L)\coloneq\limsup_{\rho\to 0^{+}}\frac{\mathfrak{m}(\ell_{x,a,A},L,Q(x,\rho))}{\rho^{d}}

for all
x\in\Omega
,
a\in\mathbb{R}^{k}
,
A,L\in\mathbb{R}^{k\times d}
, and

(3.6)

\displaystyle f_{\rm surf}(x,\zeta,\theta,\nu)\coloneq\limsup_{\rho\to 0^{+}}\frac{\mathfrak{m}(v_{x,\zeta,\theta,\nu},0,Q_{\nu}(x,\rho))}{\rho^{d-1}}

for all
x\in\Omega
,
\zeta,\theta\in\mathbb{R}^{k}
, and
\nu\in\mathbb{S}^{d-1}
, where we recall that
Q_{\nu}(x,\rho)
is a cube centred at
x
, of side-length
\rho
, and with two faces orthogonal to
\nu
, while
Q(x,\rho)\coloneq x+(-\rho/2,\rho/2)^{d}.

The following theorem constitutes our main result concerning the class
\mathfrak{F}^{p}_{\star}
. It gives an integral representation of the bulk and surface parts by means of the functions
f_{\rm bulk}
and
f_{\rm surf}
above.

Theorem 3.7

(Integral representation)
.

Let
\mathcal{F}\in\mathfrak{F}^{p}_{\star}
.
Then, for every
(g,G)\in{\rm SD}^{p}_{\star}(\Omega;\mathbb{R}^{k})
and
B\in\mathcal{B}(\Omega)
it holds

(3.7)

\displaystyle\mathcal{F}^{a}(g,G,B)=\int_{B}\!f_{\rm bulk}\big(x,g,\nabla g,G\big)\,{\rm d}x,

(3.8)

\displaystyle\mathcal{F}^{j}(g,G,B)=\int_{J_{g}\cap B}\!\!\!\!\!f_{\rm surf}\big(x,g^{+},g^{-},\nu_{g}\big)\,{\rm d}\mathcal{H}^{d-1},

where
f_{\rm bulk}
and
f_{\rm surf}
are given by (
3.5
) and (
3.6
). In particular, if
(g,G)\in{\rm SD}^{p}_{\star}(\Omega;\mathbb{R}^{k})
with
|D^{c}g|(\Omega)=0
, then for all
B\in\mathcal{B}(\Omega)
it holds that

\mathcal{F}(g,G,B)=\int_{B}\!f_{\rm bulk}\big(x,g,\nabla g,G\big)\,{\rm d}x+\int_{J_{g}\cap B}\!\!\!\!\!f_{\rm surf}\big(x,g^{+},g^{-},\nu_{g}\big)\,{\rm d}\mathcal{H}^{d-1}.

Remark 3.8
.

If
\mathcal{F}\in\mathfrak{F}^{p}_{\star}
is invariant under translation, the functions
f_{\rm bulk}
and
f_{\rm surf}
have simpler expressions. More precisely, if

(3.9)

\mathcal{F}(g+a,G,B)=\mathcal{F}(g,G,B)

for all
(g,G)\in{\rm SD}^{p}_{\star}(\Omega;\mathbb{R}^{k})
,
a\in\mathbb{R}^{k}
, and
B\in\mathcal{B}(\Omega)
, then

f_{\rm bulk}(x,a,A,L)=f_{\rm bulk}(x,0,A,L)\quad\text{ and }\quad f_{\rm surf}(x,\zeta,\theta,\nu)=f_{\rm surf}(x,\zeta-\theta,0,\nu)

for all
x\in\Omega
,
a\in\mathbb{R}^{k}
,
A,L\in\mathbb{R}^{k\times d}
,
\zeta,\theta\in\mathbb{R}^{k}
, and
\nu\in\mathbb{S}^{d-1}
. To simplify the notation, when the invariance property (
3.9
) holds, we will write
f_{\rm bulk}(x,A,L)
and
f_{\rm surf}(x,\zeta-\theta,\nu)
in place of
f_{\rm bulk}(x,0,A,L)
and
f_{\rm surf}(x,\zeta-\theta,0,\nu)
.

Relaxation of bulk-surface energies.
Our goal is to employ the integral representation result in Theorem
3.7
to prove a relaxation result for energies defined on
{\rm SBV}(\Omega;\mathbb{R}^{k})
, in the spirit of the seminal work
[
25
]
. More precisely, we consider a bulk integrand
\Psi\colon\mathbb{R}^{d}\times\mathbb{R}^{k\times d}\to[0,+\infty)
and a surface integrand
\gamma\colon\mathbb{R}^{d}\times\mathbb{R}^{k}\times\mathbb{S}^{d-1}\to[0,+\infty)
, and the associated integral functional
\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathcal{E}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\colon{\rm SBV}(\Omega;\mathbb{R}^{k})\times\mathcal{B}(\Omega)\to[0,+\infty)
defined by

\mathcal{E}(u,B)\coloneq\int_{B}\Psi(x,\nabla u)\,{\rm d}x+\int_{J_{u}\cap B}\gamma(x,[u],\nu_{u})\,{\rm d}\mathcal{H}^{d-1}

for all
(u,B)\in{\rm SBV}(\Omega;\mathbb{R}^{k})\times\mathcal{B}(\Omega)
.

Under suitable hypotheses on
\Psi
and
\gamma
specified below, we study the functional

(3.10)

I^{p}_{\star}(g,G)\coloneq\inf\Big\{\liminf_{n\to+\infty}\mathcal{E}(u_{n},\Omega)\colon\,\{u_{n}\}_{n}\subset{\rm SBV}(\Omega;\mathbb{R}^{k})\text{ with }\color[rgb]{0,0,0}u_{n}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathbin{{\mathop{\to}\limits}}(g,G)\text{ in }\Omega\Big\}

for all
(g,G)\in{\rm SD}^{p}_{\star}(\Omega;\mathbb{R}^{k})
. To this end,
for
O\in\mathcal{O}(\Omega)
we consider the localised functional
I^{p}_{\star}(\cdot,\cdot,O)
defined for all
(g,G)\in{\rm SD}^{p}_{\star}(\Omega;\mathbb{R}^{k})
by

(3.11)

\hskip-8.5359ptI^{p}_{\star}(g,G,O)\coloneq\inf\Big\{\liminf_{n\to+\infty}\mathcal{E}(u_{n},O)\colon\,\{u_{n}\}_{n}\subset{\rm SBV}(O;\mathbb{R}^{k})\text{ with }u_{n}\mathbin{{\mathop{\to}\limits}}(g,G)\text{ in }O\Big\}.

We then extend this definition to every Borel set
B\subset\Omega
by setting

(3.12)

\hskip-8.5359ptI^{p}_{\star}(g,G,B)\coloneq\inf\big\{I^{p}_{\star}(g,G,O)\colon\,O\supset B\text{ with }O\in\mathcal{O}(\Omega)\big\}.

It will be shown in Theorem
3.13
that, under suitable hypotheses on
\Psi
and
\gamma
, the set function
O\mapsto I^{p}_{\star}(g,G,O)
given by (
3.11
) coincides with the trace on
\mathcal{O}(\Omega)
of a bounded Radon measure, so that definitions (
3.11
) and (
3.12
) agree on
\mathcal{O}(\Omega)
.

We now introduce the sets of assumptions we will make on
\Psi
and
\gamma
. Whereas the assumptions on
\Psi
are modelled after those in
[
25
]
, for the surface density we fundamentally depart from the usual assumption of 1-homogeneous densities by considering bounded densities which are close to 1-homogeneous functions at the origin. We assume that
\Psi
satisfies the following properties:

(
\Psi
1)

(measurability)
\Psi
is a Carathéodory function;

(
\Psi
2)

(Lipschitz continuity) there is a constant
C_{\Psi}>0
such that

|\Psi(x,A_{1})-\Psi(x,A_{2})|\leq C_{\Psi}|A_{1}-A_{2}|\big(1+|A_{1}|^{p-1}+|A_{2}|^{p-1}\big)

for
\mathcal{L}^{d}
-a.e.
x\in\Omega
and all
A_{1},A_{2}\in\mathbb{R}^{k\times d}
;

(
\Psi
3)

(control from above) there is
A_{0}\in\mathbb{R}^{k\times d}
such that

\Psi(\cdot,A_{0})\in L^{\infty}(\Omega)
;

(
\Psi
4)

(lower bound) there is a constant
c_{\Psi}>0
such that

c_{\Psi}|A|^{p}-\frac{1}{c_{\Psi}}\leq\Psi(x,A)

for
\mathcal{L}^{d}
-a.e.
x\in\Omega
and all
A\in\mathbb{R}^{k\times d}
;

(
\Psi
5)

(continuity in
x
) there is a continuous function
\omega_{\Psi}\colon[0,+\infty)\to[0,+\infty)
, with
\omega_{\Psi}(0)=0
, such that for every
x_{1},x_{2}\in\Omega
and
A\in\mathbb{R}^{k\times d}
it holds

|\Psi(x_{1},A)-\Psi(x_{2},A)|\leq\omega_{\Psi}(|x_{1}-x_{2}|)\big(\Psi(x_{1},A)+\Psi(x_{2},A)\big).

Remark 3.9
.

Any function
\Psi
satisfying
(

Ψ

2)
and
(

Ψ

3)
also satisfies the following property:

(
\Psi
3’)

(upper bound) there exists a constant
\overline{C}_{\Psi}>0
such that

(3.13)

\Psi(x,A)\leq\overline{C}_{\Psi}\big(|A|^{p}+1)

for
\mathcal{L}^{d}
-a.e.
x\in\Omega
and all
A\in\mathbb{R}^{k\times d}.

Indeed, letting
A_{0}
be the matrix of
(

Ψ

3)
, we may use
(

Ψ

2)
to obtain for all
A\in\mathbb{R}^{k\times d}

\Psi(x,A)\leq\Psi(x,A_{0})+C_{\Psi}(|A|+|A_{0}|+|A|^{p}+|A_{0}|^{p}+|A||A_{0}|^{p-1}+|A_{0}||A|^{p-1}).

In light of
(

Ψ

3)
, this inequality implies (
3.13
) for
\mathcal{L}^{d}
-a.e.
x\in\Omega
and for all
A\in\mathbb{R}^{k\times d}
, for a sufficiently large constant
\overline{C}_{\Psi}
depending only on
A_{0}
,
C_{\Psi}
,
\Omega
, and
p
.

In the following list we collect the assumptions we make on the surface integrand
\gamma
:

(
\gamma
1)

(symmetry) for every
x\in\Omega
,
\zeta\in\mathbb{R}^{k}
, and

\nu\in\mathbb{S}^{d-1}
it holds

\gamma(x,\zeta,\nu)=\gamma(x,-\zeta,-\nu);

(
\gamma
2)

(upper and lower bounds) there are two constants
c_{\gamma},C_{\gamma}>0
such that for all
x\in\Omega
,
\zeta\in\mathbb{R}^{k}
, and
\nu\in\mathbb{S}^{d-1}
it holds

c_{\gamma}(|\zeta|\wedge 1)\leq\gamma(x,\zeta,\nu)\leq C_{\gamma}(|\zeta|\wedge 1);

(
\gamma
3)

(sub-additivity) for all
x\in\Omega
,
\zeta_{1}
,
\zeta_{2}\in\mathbb{R}^{k}
, and
\nu\in\mathbb{S}^{d-1}
it holds

\gamma(x,\zeta_{1}+\zeta_{2},\nu)\leq\gamma(x,\zeta_{1},\nu)+\gamma(x,\zeta_{2},\nu);

(
\gamma
4)

(continuity in
x
) there is a continuous function
\omega_{\gamma}\colon[0,+\infty)\to[0,+\infty)
, with
\omega_{\gamma}(0)=0
, such that for every
x_{1},x_{2}\in\Omega
,
\zeta\in\mathbb{R}^{k}
, and
\nu\in\mathbb{S}^{d-1}
it holds

|\gamma(x_{1},\zeta,\nu)-\gamma(x_{2},\zeta,\nu)|\leq\omega_{\gamma}(|x_{1}-x_{2}|)(|\zeta|\land 1);

(
\gamma
5)

(quasi-monotonicity) there exists a constant
\beta_{\gamma}\geq 1
such that for every
x\in\Omega
and
\nu\in\mathbb{S}^{d-1}
it holds

\gamma(x,\zeta_{1},\nu)\leq\gamma(x,\zeta_{2},\nu)

whenever
\beta_{\gamma}|\zeta_{1}|\leq|\zeta_{2}|
;

(
\gamma
6)

(rate of growth at the origin) there exists a continuous nondecreasing function
\vartheta\colon[0,+\infty)\to[0,+\infty)
, with
\vartheta(0)=0
and
\vartheta(h)\geq\frac{c_{\gamma}}{C_{\gamma}}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}h-1
for all
h\geq 0
, with the constants
c_{\gamma},C_{\gamma}>0
of
(

γ

2)
, such that for all
x\in\Omega,

\zeta\in\mathbb{R}^{k}
, and
\nu\in\mathbb{S}^{d-1}
the limit

(3.14)

\gamma^{0}(x,\zeta,\nu)\coloneq\lim_{s\to 0^{+}}\frac{1}{s}\gamma(x,s\zeta,\nu)

exists and satisfies

\big|\frac{1}{s}\gamma(x,s\zeta,\nu)-\gamma^{0}(x,\zeta,\nu)\big|\leq\vartheta(s|\zeta|)\frac{1}{s}\gamma(x,s\zeta,\nu)

for all
s>0
.

Remark 3.10

(Assumptions
(

γ

1)
–
(

γ

5)
)
.

Properties
(

γ

1)
and
(

γ

3)
are standard assumptions on surface densities, while the growth condition
(

γ

2)
is a core ingredient in our study, see (
1.3
) in the introduction for the motivation. The continuity condition
(

γ

4)
is also a standard assumption, slightly adjusted to our setting by using
(|\zeta|\land 1)
in place of
|\zeta|
. Property
(

γ

5)
was introduced in
[
19
]
and was later employed in several other works (see, for instance,
[
20
,
41
,
32
,
30
]
). In that paper, it is also shown (see
[
19
, Remark 3.2]
and
[
30
, Remark 3.4]
) that this property is weaker than monotonicity in
|\zeta|
of
\gamma
.

Remark 3.11

(Assumption
(

γ

6)
)
.

A condition closely related to
(

γ

6)
was already considered in
[
21
, Remark 3.5]
or
[
25
, Remark 3.3]
, and was first introduced in this exact form in
[
34
,
30
]
, see in particular
[
30
, (4.9)]
. In
[
34
, Remark 4.7]
and
[
30
, Remark 6.5]
it is noted that condition
\vartheta(h)\geq\frac{c_{\gamma}}{C_{\gamma}}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}h-1
for all
h\geq 0
is necessary and sufficient for the existence of functions
\gamma
satisfying condition
(

γ

6)
. We also observe that
\gamma^{0}
is positively
1
-homogeneous, i.e.,

(3.15)

\gamma^{0}(x,t\zeta,\nu)=t\gamma^{0}(x,\zeta,\nu)

for all
x\in\Omega
,
\zeta\in\mathbb{R}^{k}
,
\nu\in\mathbb{S}^{d-1}
, and
t\geq 0
. Note that, together with
(

γ

2)
, (
3.15
) implies

(3.16)

c_{\gamma}|\zeta|\leq\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\gamma^{0}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}(x,\zeta,\nu)\leq C_{\gamma}|\zeta|

for all
x\in\Omega
,
\zeta\in\mathbb{R}^{k}
, and
\nu\in\mathbb{S}^{d-1}
.

Remark 3.12

(Properties of
\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\gamma^{0}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}
)
.

The integrand
\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\gamma^{0}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}
satisfies properties closely related to
(

γ

3)
–
(

γ

4)
. Indeed, it follows directly from
(

γ

3)
and
(

γ

6)
that
\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\gamma^{0}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}
satisfies
(

γ

3)
with
\gamma
replaced by
\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\gamma^{0}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}
, while
(

γ

4)
and
(

γ

6)
imply that

(3.17)

|\gamma^{0}(x_{1},\zeta,\nu)-\gamma^{0}(x_{2},\zeta,\nu)|\leq\omega_{\gamma}(|x_{1}-x_{2}|)|\zeta|

for all
x_{1},x_{2}\in\Omega
,
\zeta\in\mathbb{R}^{k}
, and
\nu\in\mathbb{S}^{d-1}
.

The following is the first of the three results concerning the integral representation of (
3.10
). Note that we do not assume
(

Ψ

5)
and
(

γ

5)
–
(

γ

6)
nor put any restrictions on the value of
p
.

Theorem 3.13

(Relaxation I)
.

Let
\Psi
and
\gamma
be integrands satisfying assumptions
(

Ψ

1)
–
(

Ψ

4)
and
(

γ

1)
–
(

γ

4)
.
Then
(
up to a shift
)
the functional
I^{p}_{\star}
defined by (
3.12
) belongs to
\mathfrak{F}^{p}_{\star}
and for all
(g,G)\in{\rm SD}^{p}_{\star}(\Omega;\mathbb{R}^{k})
and
B\in\mathcal{B}(\Omega)
it holds

(3.18)

\displaystyle(I^{p}_{\star})^{a}(g,G,B)=\int_{B}\!f_{\rm bulk}\big(x,\nabla g,G\big)\,{\rm d}x,

(3.19)

\displaystyle(I^{p}_{\star})^{j}(g,G,B)=\int_{J_{g}\cap B}\!\!\!\!\!f_{\rm surf}\big(x,[g],\nu_{g}\big)\,{\rm d}\mathcal{H}^{d-1},

where
f_{\rm bulk}
and
f_{\rm surf}
are given by (
3.5
) and (
3.6
), replacing
\mathcal{F}
with
I^{p}_{\star}

(
see also Remark
3.8
)
. In particular, if
(g,G)\in{\rm SD}^{p}_{\star}(\Omega;\mathbb{R}^{k})
with
|D^{c}g|(\Omega)=0
, then for all
B\in\mathcal{B}(\Omega)
it holds that

I^{p}_{\star}(g,G,B)=\int_{B}\!f_{\rm bulk}\big(x,\nabla g,G\big)\,{\rm d}x+\int_{J_{g}\cap B}\!\!\!\!\!f_{\rm surf}\big(x,[g],\nu_{g}\big)\,{\rm d}\mathcal{H}^{d-1}.

Our second main result gives a more explicit expression for the densities
f_{\rm bulk}
and
f_{\rm surf}
appearing in (
3.18
) and (
3.19
) when
p>1
. To present the result, we first introduce some further notation. For
\zeta\in\mathbb{R}^{k}
and
\nu\in\mathbb{S}^{d-1}
we set
v_{\zeta,\nu}\coloneq v_{0,\zeta,0,\nu}
(see (
3.4
)).
Given
A,L\in\mathbb{R}^{k\times d}
,
\zeta\in\mathbb{R}^{k}
, and
\nu\in\mathbb{S}^{d-1}
, we consider the classes

(3.20)

\displaystyle\mathcal{C}_{p,\star}^{\rm bulk}(A,L)\coloneq\Big\{u\in{\rm SBV}(Q;\mathbb{R}^{k})\colon\,u=\ell_{0,0,A}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}

\displaystyle\text{ near $\partial Q$},\ \int_{Q}\nabla u\,{\rm d}x=L,\ \|\nabla u\|_{L^{p}(Q)}^{p}<+\infty\Big\}

and

(3.21)

\hskip-2.84544pt\mathcal{C}_{p,\star}^{\rm surf}(\zeta,\nu)\coloneq\Big\{u\in{\rm SBV}(Q_{\nu};\mathbb{R}^{k})\colon\,u=v_{\zeta,\nu}\text{ near $\partial Q_{\nu}$\color[rgb]{0,0,0}},\ \nabla u=0\ \text{ $\mathcal{L}^{d}$-a.e.\ on \color[rgb]{0,0,0}$Q_{\nu}$}\Big\}.

We set

(3.22)

H_{p}(x,A,L)\coloneq\inf_{u\in\mathcal{C}_{p,\star}^{\rm bulk}(A,L)}\Big\{\int_{Q}\Psi\big(x,\nabla u(y)\big)\,{\rm d}y+\int_{J_{u}\cap Q}\gamma^{0}\big(x,[u](y),\nu_{u}(y)\big)\,{\rm d}\mathcal{H}^{d-1}(y)\Big\}

for all
x\in\Omega
and
A,L\in\mathbb{R}^{k\times d}
, and

(3.23)

h_{p}(x,\zeta,\nu)\coloneq\inf_{u\in\mathcal{C}_{p,\star}^{\rm surf}(\zeta,\nu)}\Big\{\!\int_{J_{u}\cap Q_{\nu}}\gamma\big(x,[u](y),\nu_{u}(y)\big)\,{\rm d}\mathcal{H}^{d-1}(y)\Big\}

for all
x\in\Omega
,
\zeta\in\mathbb{R}^{k}
, and
\nu\in\mathbb{S}^{d-1}
.
Note that, in contrast to (
3.23
), in (
3.22
) the density
\gamma^{0}
appears which is the relevant quantity here as affine boundary conditions are effectively approximated by infinitesimally small jumps, cf. also
[
25
, Remark 3.3]
.

The following constitutes a characterisation of
(I^{p}_{\star})^{a}
and
(I^{p}_{\star})^{j}
in terms of the densities
H_{p}
and
h_{p}
.
We remark that the restriction
p>1
is due to the nature of the truncation techniques we employ (see Lemma
6.3
), which are ill-behaved when
p=1
. This is due to concentration phenomena that may occur when dealing with the weak
∗
-convergence of measures.

Theorem 3.14

(Relaxation II)
.

Let
p>1
and let
\Psi
and
\gamma
be integrands satisfying assumptions
(

Ψ

1)
–
(

Ψ

5)
and
(

γ

1)
–
(

γ

6)
. Then, for all
(g,G)\in{\rm SD}^{p}_{\star}(\Omega;\mathbb{R}^{k})
and
B\in\mathcal{B}(\Omega)
equalities (
3.18
) and (
3.19
) hold with
f_{\rm bulk}(x,\nabla g,G)
and
f_{\rm surf}(x,[g],\nu_{g})
replaced by
H_{p}(x,\nabla g,G)
and
h_{p}(x,[g],\nu_{g})
, where
H_{p}
and
h_{p}
are the functions defined by (
3.22
) and (
3.23
), respectively.

Proposition 3.15

(Properties of
H_{p}
and
h_{p}
)
.

Let
p>1
and let
\Psi
and
\gamma
be integrands satisfying assumptions
(

Ψ

1)
–
(

Ψ

5)
and
(

γ

1)
–
(

γ

6)
. Given
L\in\mathbb{R}^{k\times d}
, let
H_{p}^{L}\colon\Omega\times\mathbb{R}^{k\times d}\to[0,+\infty)
be the function defined for
x\in\Omega
and
A\in\mathbb{R}^{k\times d}
by
H^{L}_{p}(x,A)\coloneq H_{p}(x,A,L)
. Then
H^{L}_{p}
satisfies
(

Ψ

1)
–
(

Ψ

5)

with
p
replaced by
1
, while
h_{p}
satisfies
(

γ

1)
–
(

γ

5)
.

Moreover, for every
x\in\Omega
and
A\in\mathbb{R}^{k\times d}
there exists a constant
C>0
, depending only on
x
and on
A
, such that

|H_{p}(x,A,L_{1})-H_{p}(x,A,L_{2})|\leq C|L_{1}-L_{2}|(1+|L_{1}|^{p-1}+|L_{2}|^{p-1})\quad\text{ for all $L_{1},L_{2}\in\mathbb{R}^{k\times d}$}.

Finally, there exist constants
c_{H},C_{H}>0
such that

c_{H}(|A|+|L|^{p})-\frac{1}{c_{H}}\leq H_{p}(x,A,L)\leq C_{H}(|A|+|L|^{p}+1)

for all
x\in\Omega
and
A,L\in\mathbb{R}^{k\times d}
.

The final result of this paper provides a complete integral representation for
I^{p}_{\star}
, including its Cantor part
(I^{p}_{\star})^{c}
. This comes upon assuming a stronger hypothesis on
\Psi
and
\gamma
, namely that
\Psi
and
\gamma
are independent of the
x
-variable. This hypothesis ensures that the densities
H_{p}
and
h_{p}
are independent of
x
, and that
I^{p}_{\star}
satisfies the following invariance property: for every
(g,G)\in{\rm SD}^{p}_{\star}(\Omega;\mathbb{R}^{k})
and
x\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
, it holds

(3.24)

I^{p}_{\star}(g(\cdot-x),G(\cdot-x),x+B)=I^{p}_{\star}(g,G,B)

for every
B\in\mathcal{B}(\Omega)
with
x+B\subset\Omega
. This property makes possible to rely on the integral representation of Bouchitté, Fonseca, & Mascarenhas
[
18
, Theorem 3.12]
for functionals defined on
{\rm BV}(\Omega;\mathbb{R}^{k})
.

Theorem 3.16

(Relaxation III)
.

Let
p>1
and let
\Psi
and
\gamma
be
x
-independent integrands satisfying assumptions
(

Ψ

1)
–
(

Ψ

5)
and
(

γ

1)
–
(

γ

6)
. Then, for every
(g,G)\in{\rm SD}_{\star}^{p}(\Omega;\mathbb{R}^{k})
and
B\in\mathcal{B}(\Omega)
we have

I^{p}_{\star}(g,G,B)=\int_{B}H_{p}(\nabla g,G)\,{\rm d}x+\int_{B}H_{p}^{\infty}\Big(\frac{{\rm d}D^{c}g}{{\rm d}|D^{c}g|},0\Big)\,{\rm d}|D^{c}g|+\int_{J_{g}\cap B}h_{p}([g],\nu_{g})\,{\rm d}\mathcal{H}^{d-1},

where
H_{p}
and
h_{p}
are the functions defined by (
3.22
) and (
3.23
) without
x
-dependence, and
H_{p}^{\infty}(\cdot,0)
denotes the recession function of
H_{p}(\cdot,0)
, i.e., the function defined for all
A\in\mathbb{R}^{k\times d}
by

(3.25)

H_{p}^{\infty}(A,0)\coloneq\limsup_{t\to+\infty}\frac{H_{p}(tA,0)}{t}.

Remark 3.17

(Compactness)
.

Relying on arguments first devised in
[
41
]
, some sufficient conditions for coerciveness of integral functionals on
{\rm GBV}_{\star}(\Omega;\mathbb{R}^{k})
were proved in
[
32
, Theorem 5.5]
and
[
39
, Theorem 4.8]
. More precisely, in those papers the authors consider open sets
\Omega
,
\widetilde{\Omega}
, with Lipschitz boundary satisfying
\Omega\subset\widetilde{\Omega}
, a boundary datum
w\in W^{1,1}(\widetilde{\Omega};\mathbb{R}^{k})
, and the functional
\mathcal{F}\colon{\rm GBV}_{\star}(\widetilde{\Omega};\mathbb{R}^{k})\to[0,+\infty)
defined by

\mathcal{F}(u)\coloneq\begin{cases}\displaystyle\int_{\widetilde{\Omega}}f(\nabla u)\,{\rm d}x+\int_{\widetilde{\Omega}}f^{\infty}\Big(\frac{{\rm d}D^{c}u}{{\rm d}|D^{c}u|}\Big)\,{\rm d}|D^{c}u|+\int_{J_{u}\cap\widetilde{\Omega}}g([u],\nu_{u})\,{\rm d}\mathcal{H}^{d-1}&\text{if $u=w$ on $\widetilde{\Omega}\setminus\Omega$},\\
+\infty&\text{otherwise},\end{cases}

for some Borel functions
f\colon\mathbb{R}^{k\times d}\to[0,+\infty)
and
g\colon\mathbb{R}^{k}\times\mathbb{S}^{d-1}\to[0,+\infty)
, with
f
satisfying

\displaystyle c_{F}|A|-\frac{1}{c_{F}}\leq f(A)\leq C_{F}|A|+C_{F}\quad\text{for all $A\in\mathbb{R}^{k\times d}$},

for some constants
c_{F},C_{F}>0
, and with
g
satisfying
(

γ

2)
and
(

γ

5)
. Under these hypotheses, they show that all sequences of functions
\{u_{n}\}_{n}\subset{\rm GBV}_{\star}(\widetilde{\Omega};\mathbb{R}^{k})
with
\sup_{n}\mathcal{F}(u_{n})<+\infty
admit modifications
\{y_{n}\}_{n}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\subset{\rm GBV}_{\star}(\widetilde{\Omega};\mathbb{R}^{k})
such that
\mathcal{F}(y_{n})\leq\mathcal{F}(u_{n})+1/n
for all
n\in\mathbb{N}
, and such that (a subsequence of)
\{y_{n}\}_{n}
converges in
L^{0}(\widetilde{\Omega};\mathbb{R}^{k})
to some
u\in{\rm GBV}_{\star}(\widetilde{\Omega};\mathbb{R}^{k})
with
\mathcal{F}(u)<+\infty
.

Taking into account some minor modifications due to the presence of the matrix variable
G
,
these compactness results readily adapt to the functional
I^{p}_{\star}
, thanks to Proposition
3.15
and Theorem
3.16
. This procedure guarantees that
I^{p}_{\star}
admits minimisers attaining the boundary condition
w
on
\widetilde{\Omega}\setminus\Omega
.

4.
Approximation Theorem in
{\rm SD}^{p}_{\star}(\Omega;\mathbb{R}^{k})
: Proof of Theorem
3.1

This section is devoted to the proof of Theorem
3.1
. The approach we follow is similar to the one in
[
49
, Theorem 1.2]
(see also
[
25
, Theorem 2.12]
), but the proof in our case is technically more demanding. To explain why, we recall that the main ingredients of the proof of
[
49
, Theorem 2.3]
are essentially three: (1) Anzellotti-Giaquinta’s Theorem
[
6
, Theorem 1]
, (2)
[
25
, Lemma 2.9]
, which states that every
u\in C^{\infty}_{c}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
can be approximated by means of piecewise constant functions whose total variation is controlled by the
L^{1}
norm of
\nabla u
, and (3) Alberti’s Theorem (see Theorem
4.5
).

4.1.
Proof of the Approximation Theorem

To proceed with this strategy, we need to prove suitable adaptations of the first two results in our functional setting, calling for nontrivial new arguments. We first give the statements of all these results and show how they allow to obtain Theorem
3.1
, postponing their proofs to the end of the section. The first result, whose proof is based on a classical result of Goffman & Serrin
[
43
, Theorem 4
′
]
, shows that, if
O\in\mathcal{O}(\Omega)
is a Lipschitz set, every

u\in{\rm BV}(O;\mathbb{R}^{k})
can be approximated by a sequence of
{\rm SBV}(O;\mathbb{R}^{k})
-functions
\{u_{n}\}_{n}
in such a way that
\mathcal{V}(u_{n},O)
converges to
\mathcal{V}(u,O)
.

Theorem 4.1

(Density result in
{\rm BV}
)
.

Let
O^{\prime},O\in\mathcal{O}(\Omega)
, with
O^{\prime}\subset\subset O
, and let
u\in{\rm BV}(O;\mathbb{R}^{k})
. Assume that
\mathcal{H}^{d-1}(J_{u}\cap\partial O^{\prime})=0.
Then there exists a sequence
\{u_{n}\}_{n}\subset{\rm SBV}(O^{\prime};\mathbb{R}^{k})\cap L^{\infty}(O^{\prime};\mathbb{R}^{k})
such that
u_{n}\to u
strongly in
L^{1}(O^{\prime};\mathbb{R}^{k})
as
n\to+\infty
and

(4.1)

\limsup_{n\to+\infty}\mathcal{V}(u_{n},O^{\prime})\leq\mathcal{V}(u,O).

Moreover, if
O
has Lipschitz boundary, then we can find another sequence, still denoted by
\{u_{n}\}_{n}
, with
\{u_{n}\}_{n}\subset{\rm SBV}(O;\mathbb{R}^{k})\cap L^{\infty}(O;\mathbb{R}^{k})
, such that
u_{n}\to u
strongly in
L^{1}(O;\mathbb{R}^{k})
as
n\to+\infty
and

(4.2)

\lim_{n\to+\infty}\mathcal{V}(u_{n},O)=\mathcal{V}(u,O).

Remark 4.2
.

(i) Replacing
L^{1}
-convergence by
L^{0}
-convergence, the statement also holds for
{\rm GBV}_{\star}
-functions by performing an additional truncation procedure, as explained at the beginning of the proof of Theorem
3.1
below.

(ii) The theorem holds in a slightly weaker form also for more general energies than
\mathcal{V}
. More precisely, the arguments in the proof can be adapted with no difficulty to energies of the form

F(u,O)\coloneq\int_{O}f(\nabla u)\,{\rm d}x+\int_{O}f^{\infty}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\Big(\frac{{\rm d}D^{c}u}{{\rm d}|D^{c}u|}\Big)\,{\rm d}|D^{c}u|+\int_{J_{u}\cap O}g([u],\nu_{u})\,{\rm d}\mathcal{H}^{d-1},

with
f\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\colon\mathbb{R}^{k\times d}\to[0,+\infty)
convex,
g\colon\mathbb{R}^{k}\times\mathbb{S}^{d-1}\to[0,+\infty)
with
\zeta\mapsto g\color[rgb]{0,0,0}(\zeta,\nu)
continuous for all
\nu\in\mathbb{S}^{d-1}
, and

f\color[rgb]{0,0,0}(A)\leq C(|A|+1)\text{ for all $A\in\mathbb{R}^{k\times d}$}\quad\text{ and }\quad g(\zeta,\nu)\leq C|\zeta|\land 1\text{ for all $\zeta\in\mathbb{R}^{k}$ and $\nu\in\mathbb{S}^{d-1}$}

for some constant
C>0
, where the recession function
f^{\infty}
is defined as in (
3.25
) with
f
in place of
H_{p}
. Indeed, this is the setting where
[
43
, Theorem 4
′
]
can still be employed. In this case, (
4.2
) has to be replaced by

\limsup_{n\to+\infty}{F}(u_{n},O)\leq{F}(u,O),

as, in general,
F
could fail to be
L^{1}(O;\mathbb{R}^{k})
-lower semicontinuous.

(iii) A careful inspection of the proof of Theorem
4.1
reveals that the approximating sequence
\{u_{n}\}_{n}
actually satisfies a property stronger than (
4.2
). More precisely, we have

\lim_{n\to+\infty}\mathcal{V}(u_{n},O\setminus J_{u_{n}})=V(u,O\setminus J_{u})\quad\text{ and }\lim_{n\to+\infty}\mathcal{V}(u_{n},J_{u_{n}})=V(u,J_{u}).

The same observation applies to the functionals
F
of (ii), with the caveat that equalities need to be replaced by inequalities if
F
is not lower semicontinuous.

The general idea is to approximate the sequence obtained in Theorem
4.1
by a sequence of piecewise constant functions. The existing results in the literature, see e.g.
[
25
, Lemma 2.9]
, are not expedient for us as they guarantee convergence of the total variation but not of the functional
\mathcal{V}
, as required in our setting.

Lemma 4.3
.

Let
O\in\mathcal{O}(\Omega)
and
u\in{\rm BV}(O;\mathbb{R}^{k})
. Then there exists a sequence of piecewise constant functions
\{u_{n}\}_{n}\subset{\rm SBV}(O;\mathbb{R}^{k})\cap L^{\infty}(O;\mathbb{R}^{k})
such that
u_{n}\to u
strongly in
L^{1}(O;\mathbb{R}^{k})
as
n\to+\infty
and

\lim_{n\to+\infty}\mathcal{V}(u_{n},O)=\mathcal{V}(u,O).

Remark 4.4
.

Replacing
L^{1}
-convergence by
L^{0}
-convergence, the statement also holds for
{\rm GBV}_{\star}
-functions by performing an additional truncation procedure, as explained at the beginning of the proof of Theorem
3.1
below.

Before proceeding with the proof of Theorem
3.1
, we recall for the reader’s convenience the statement of Alberti’s Theorem
[
1
, Theorem 3]
.

Theorem 4.5

(Alberti’s Theorem)
.

There exists a constant
C=C(d)>0
with the property that for every
O\in\mathcal{O}(\Omega)
and
G\in L^{1}(O;\mathbb{R}^{k\times d})
there exists a function
u\in{\rm SBV}(O;\mathbb{R}^{k})
such that
\nabla u=G

\mathcal{L}^{d}
-a.e. in
O
and

\int_{J_{u}}|[u]|\,{\rm d}\mathcal{H}^{d-1}\leq C\|G\|_{L^{1}(O)}.

We are ready to give the proof of Theorem
3.1
.

Proof of Theorem
3.1
.
Let us fix
(g,G)\in{\rm SD}_{\star}^{p}(O;\mathbb{R}^{k})
. By Proposition
A.3
, for every
n\in\mathbb{N}
the function
g^{n}\coloneq\Phi_{n}\circ g
(see (
A.6
)) belongs to
{\rm BV}(O;\mathbb{R}^{k})
and

\sup_{n\in\mathbb{N}}\mathcal{V}(g^{n},O)=\mathcal{V}(g,O),\quad\text{ and }\quad\{g^{n}\}_{n}\text{ converges to }g\text{ in $L^{0}(O;\mathbb{R}^{k})$ as $n\to+\infty$}.

We apply Theorem
4.1
to
g^{n}
and obtain a sequence
\{u_{\ell,n}\}_{\ell}\subset{\rm SBV}(O;\mathbb{R}^{k})\cap L^{\infty}(O;\mathbb{R}^{k})
converging to
g^{n}
strongly in
L^{1}(O;\mathbb{R}^{k})
as
\ell\to+\infty
and such that

(4.3)

\lim_{\ell\to+\infty}\mathcal{V}(u_{\ell,n},O)=\mathcal{V}(g^{n},O)\leq\mathcal{V}(g,O).

We can now use Alberti’s Theorem
4.5
to obtain a function
h\in{\rm SBV}(O;\mathbb{R}^{k})
with
\nabla h=G

\mathcal{L}^{d}
-a.e. in
O
and

(4.4)

|Dh|(O)\leq C\|G\|_{L^{1}(O)}

for a suitable constant
C
depending only on
d
. Applying Lemma
4.3
to
u_{\ell,n}-h
, we find a sequence of piecewise constant functions
\{z_{\ell,n}^{j}\}_{j}\subset{\rm SBV}(O;\mathbb{R}^{k})\cap L^{\infty}(O;\mathbb{R}^{k})
which converges to
u_{\ell,n}-h
strongly in
L^{1}(O;\mathbb{R}^{k})
as
j\to+\infty
and with

(4.5)

\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\lim_{j\to+\infty}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathcal{V}(z^{j}_{\ell,n},O)=\mathcal{V}(u_{\ell,n}-h,O)\leq C\big(\mathcal{V}(u_{\ell,n},O)+|Dh|(O)\big)

for a possibly larger constant
C
.
Next, we set
u_{\ell,n}^{j}\coloneq h+z_{\ell,n}^{j}\in{\rm SBV}(O;\mathbb{R}^{k})
and observe that by construction
\nabla u_{\ell,n}^{j}=G

\mathcal{L}^{d}
-a.e. in
O
and that
\{u_{\ell,n}^{j}\}_{j}
converges to
u_{\ell,n}
strongly in
L^{1}(O;\mathbb{R}^{k})
as
j\to+\infty
. Moreover, by (
4.3
)–(
4.5
) we have, for all
n\in\mathbb{N}
,

\limsup_{\ell\to+\infty}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\lim_{j\to+\infty}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathcal{V}(u^{j}_{\ell,n},O)\leq C\big(\mathcal{V}(g,O)+\|G\|_{L^{1}(O)}\big).

Since the
L^{0}
-convergence is metrisable, we may use a diagonal argument to obtain sequences
\{\ell_{n}\}_{n}
and
\{j_{n}\}_{n}
, with
\ell_{n}\to+\infty
and
j_{n}\to+\infty
as
n\to+\infty
, so that, setting
u_{n}\coloneq u^{j_{n}}_{\ell_{n},n}
, we have
u_{n}\to g
in
L^{0}(O;\mathbb{R}^{k})
and

\limsup_{n\to+\infty}\mathcal{V}(u_{n},O)\leq C\Big(\mathcal{V}(g,O)+\|G\|_{L^{1}(O)}\Big).

This concludes the proof.∎

4.2.
Proof of the density results

In the rest of the section, we prove Theorem
4.1
and Lemma
4.3
.

Proof of Theorem
4.1
.
Thanks to a truncation argument based on Proposition
A.3
, we may assume that
u\in L^{\infty}(O;\mathbb{R}^{k}\color[rgb]{0,0,0})
. Moreover, it is not restrictive to assume
k=1
because we can argue componentwise. We begin by proving that there exists
\{u_{n}\}_{n}\subset{\rm SBV}(O^{\prime})\cap L^{\infty}(O^{\prime})
converging to
u
strongly in
L^{1}(O^{\prime})
such that (
4.1
) holds.

Since
J_{u}\cap O^{\prime}
is
(\mathcal{H}^{d-1},d-1)
-rectifiable, there exist a sequence
\{K_{\ell}\}_{\ell}
of pairwise disjoint compact sets of
J_{u}\cap O^{\prime}
,
a sequence
\{M_{\ell}\}_{\ell}
of
(d-1)
-dimensional orientable
C^{1}
-manifolds, with
K_{\ell}\subset\subset M_{\ell}\subset\subset O^{\prime}
for every
\ell\in\mathbb{N}
, and a Borel set
N\subset O^{\prime}
, with
\mathcal{H}^{d-1}(N)=0
, such that
J_{u}\cap O^{\prime}=N\cup\big(\bigcup_{\ell\in\mathbb{N}}K_{\ell}\big)
.
Let
\nu_{M_{\ell}}
be a continuous unit normal to
M_{\ell}
.
It is not restrictive to assume that for every
\ell\in\mathbb{N}
there exist
\sigma_{\ell}>0
and
\nu_{\ell}\in\mathbb{S}^{d-1}
, with
\nu_{\ell}\cdot\nu_{M_{\ell}}>0
on
M_{\ell}
, such that
y+t\nu_{\ell}\in O^{\prime}\setminus M_{\ell}
for every
y\in M_{\ell}
and for every
t\in(-\sigma_{\ell},0)\cup(0,\sigma_{\ell})
.

Step 1.
(Approximation away from the jump set) Let us fix
\varepsilon>0
. There exists
\ell_{\varepsilon}\in\mathbb{N}
such that, setting
K^{\varepsilon}\coloneq\bigcup_{\ell=1}^{\ell_{\varepsilon}}K_{\ell}
and
N^{\varepsilon}\coloneq\big(\bigcup_{\ell>\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\ell_{\varepsilon}}K_{\ell}\big)\cup N
, we have

(4.6)

J_{u}\cap O^{\prime}=K^{\varepsilon}\cup N^{\varepsilon}\quad\text{ and }\quad\int_{N^{\varepsilon}}|[u]|\,{\rm d}\mathcal{H}^{d-1}\leq\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\varepsilon.

Moreover, we may assume that there exists
\eta^{\varepsilon}>0
such that
\text{dist}(M_{j},M_{\ell})>\eta^{\varepsilon}
for every
j,\ell\in\{1,\dots,\ell_{\varepsilon}\}
with
j\neq\ell
and that
\sigma_{\ell}\leq\frac{\eta^{\varepsilon}}{2}
for every
\ell\in\{1,\dots,\ell_{\varepsilon}\}
. For technical reasons, it will be convenient to introduce
(d-1)
-dimensional manifolds
M^{\prime}_{\ell}
,
M^{\prime\prime}_{\ell}
such that
K_{\ell}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\subset\subset M_{\ell}^{\prime\prime}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\subset\subset M_{\ell}^{\prime}\subset\subset M_{\ell}
. For every
\sigma>0
we define

M_{\ell}(\sigma)\coloneq\big\{y+t\nu_{\ell}\colon\,y\in M_{\ell}\,\,\text{ and }\,\,t\in(-\sigma,\sigma)\big\}

and give a similar definition for
M^{\prime}_{\ell}(\sigma)
and
M^{\prime\prime}_{\ell}(\sigma)
. We also
set

(4.7)

M^{\varepsilon}(\sigma)\coloneq\bigcup_{\ell=1}^{\ell_{\varepsilon}}M_{\ell}^{\prime}(\sigma)

Later we will send
\sigma\to 0
. For the moment, we choose a fixed
\sigma
with

(4.8)

0<\sigma<\sigma^{\varepsilon}\coloneq\min\big\{\sigma_{\ell}/4\colon\,\ell\in\{1,\dots,\ell_{\varepsilon}\}\big\},

and observe that with this choice we have
M_{\ell}(4\sigma)\subset\subset O^{\prime}
for every
\ell\in\{1,\dots,\ell_{\varepsilon}\}
and
M_{j}(4\sigma)\cap M_{\ell}(4\sigma)=\mathchar 31\relax
for every
j,\ell\in\{1,\dots,\ell_{\varepsilon}\}
with
j\neq\ell
. Moreover, we have that
\tau_{\ell}\coloneq\text{dist}(K_{\ell},O^{\prime}\setminus M^{\prime}_{\ell}(\sigma))>0
. We refer to Figures
1
and
2
for an illustration.

O
O^{\prime}
\nu_{1}

Figure 1.
The sets
M^{\varepsilon}(\sigma)
and

for
\ell_{\varepsilon}=3
.

We consider a sequence of convolution kernels
\{\rho_{n}\}_{n}\subset C^{\infty}_{c}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
with
\text{supp}(\rho_{n})\subset B(0,\frac{1}{n})
and define
v_{n}\coloneq u*\rho_{n}
. Observe that, if
O^{\prime\prime}\in\mathcal{O}(O)
is such that
O^{\prime}\subset\subset O^{\prime\prime}\subset\subset O
,
x\in O^{\prime}\setminus M^{\varepsilon}(\sigma)
, and if
\frac{1}{n}\leq\min\big\{\text{dist}(O^{\prime},\mathbb{R}^{d}\setminus O^{\prime\prime}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}),\min\{\tau_{\ell}\colon\,\ell\in\{1,\dots,\ell_{\varepsilon}\}\}\big\}
, then
B(x,\frac{1}{n})\subset O^{\prime\prime}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\setminus K^{\varepsilon}
. Since
v_{n}\coloneq u*\rho_{n}
, using a result of Goffman & Serrin
[
43
, Theorem 4
′
]
and (
4.6
) we may estimate

\displaystyle\limsup_{n\to+\infty}\mathcal{V}(v_{n},O^{\prime}\setminus M^{\varepsilon}(\sigma))

\displaystyle=\limsup_{n\to+\infty}\int_{O^{\prime}\setminus M^{\varepsilon}(\sigma)}|\nabla v_{n}|\,{\rm d}x\leq\int_{O^{\prime\prime}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}}|\nabla u|\,{\rm d}x+|D^{s}u|(O^{\prime\prime}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\setminus K^{\varepsilon})

\displaystyle=\mathcal{V}(u,O^{\prime\prime}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\setminus J_{u})+\int_{N^{\varepsilon}\cup(J_{u}\cap(O^{\prime\prime}\setminus O^{\prime}))\color[rgb]{0,0,0}}|[u]|\,{\rm d}\mathcal{H}^{d-1}

\displaystyle\leq\mathcal{V}(u,O\setminus J_{u})+\int_{J_{u}\cap(O^{\prime\prime}\setminus O^{\prime})\color[rgb]{0,0,0}}|[u]|\,{\rm d}\mathcal{H}^{d-1}+\varepsilon.\color[rgb]{0,0,0}

As
O^{\prime\prime}
was arbitrary and we assumed that
\mathcal{H}^{d-1}(J_{u}\cap\partial O^{\prime})=0
, we may let
O^{\prime\prime}\searrow O^{\prime}
and deduce that

(4.9)

\limsup_{n\to+\infty}\mathcal{V}(v_{n},O^{\prime}\setminus M^{\varepsilon}(\sigma))\leq\mathcal{V}(u,O\setminus J_{u})+\varepsilon.

Step 2.
(Construction at the jump set)
We introduce the function
\phi^{\sigma}\colon[-3\sigma,3\sigma]\to[-3\sigma,-\sigma]\cup[\sigma,3\sigma]
defined by

\phi^{\sigma}(t)\coloneq\begin{cases}\frac{t}{2}-\frac{3}{2}\sigma&\text{ if }-3\sigma\leq t\leq-\sigma,\\
t-\sigma&\text{ if }-\sigma\leq t\leq 0,\\
t+\sigma&\text{ if }0<t\leq\sigma,\\
\frac{t}{2}+\frac{3}{2}\sigma&\text{ if }\sigma\leq t\leq 3\sigma,\end{cases}

and consider the function defined on
M^{\prime}_{\ell}(3\sigma)
by

v_{n}^{\sigma,\ell}(y+t\nu_{\ell})\coloneq v_{n}(y+\phi^{\sigma}(t)\nu_{\ell})\quad\text{ for every }y\in M^{\prime}_{\ell}\text{ and }t\in[-3\sigma,3\sigma].

We observe that
v_{n}^{\sigma,\ell}=v_{n}
on the top and bottom parts of the boundary (see Figure
2
) of
M^{\prime}_{\ell}(3\sigma)
, given by
M^{\prime}_{\ell}\pm 3\sigma\nu_{\ell}
. To deal with the lateral part of the boundary, namely
\partial M^{\prime}_{\ell}+[-3\sigma,3\sigma]\nu_{\ell}
, we consider a further cut-off function
\gamma_{\ell}\in C^{1}_{c}(M^{\prime}_{\ell};[0,1])
with
\gamma_{\ell}=1
in the neighbourhood
M^{\prime\prime}_{\ell}
(recall that
K_{\ell}\subset\subset M^{\prime\prime}_{\ell}\subset\subset M^{\prime}_{\ell}
). We set

(4.10)

u_{n}^{\sigma,\ell}(y+t\nu_{\ell})\coloneq\gamma_{\ell}(y)v_{n}^{\sigma,\ell}(y+t\nu_{\ell})+(1-\gamma_{\ell}(y))v_{n}(y+t\nu_{\ell})\quad\text{ for all \color[rgb]{0,0,0}}y\in M^{\prime}_{\ell}\text{ and }t\in[-3\sigma,3\sigma].

Since
u_{n}^{\sigma,\ell}=v_{n}
on the full boundary of
M^{\prime}_{\ell}(3\sigma)
, we can define a function
u^{\sigma,\varepsilon}_{n}
on
O^{\prime}
by setting

(4.11)

u^{\sigma,\varepsilon}_{n}\coloneq\begin{cases}u^{\sigma,\ell}_{n}\quad&\text{ on }M^{\prime}_{\ell}(3\sigma)\,\,\text{ for $\ell\in\{1,\dots,\ell_{\varepsilon}\}$},\\
v_{n}&\text{ on }O^{\prime}\setminus M^{\varepsilon}(3\sigma).\end{cases}

It follows directly from the definition that
u_{n}^{\sigma,\varepsilon}\in{\rm SBV}(O^{\prime})\cap L^{\infty}(O^{\prime})
, that
u_{n}^{\sigma,\varepsilon}
is a smooth Lipschitz function on
O^{\prime}\setminus\big(\bigcup_{\ell=1}^{\ell_{\varepsilon}}M^{\prime}_{\ell}\big)
, and that for every
n\in\mathbb{N}
we have

(4.12)

\displaystyle J_{u_{n}^{\sigma,\varepsilon}}\subset\bigcup_{\ell=1}^{\ell_{\varepsilon}}M^{\prime}_{\ell}\subset\subset O^{\prime},

\displaystyle\nu_{u_{n}^{\sigma,\varepsilon}}=\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\nu_{M_{\ell}}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\quad\mathcal{H}^{d-1}\text{-a.e. on }M^{\prime}_{\ell}\cap J_{u_{n}^{\sigma,\varepsilon}},

(4.13)

\displaystyle[u_{n}^{\sigma,\varepsilon}]=\big(v_{n}(\cdot+\sigma\nu_{\ell})-v_{n}(\cdot-\sigma\nu_{\ell})\big)\gamma_{\ell}\quad\text{on }M^{\prime}_{\ell}\cap J_{u_{n}^{\sigma,\varepsilon}},

where we have used
M_{\ell}\subset O^{\prime}
, the inequality
\nu_{\ell}\cdot\nu_{M_{\ell}}>0
, and the definitions of
\phi^{\sigma}
and
v_{n}^{\sigma,\ell}
, see Figure
2
.

Step 3.
(
L^{1}
-convergence) We estimate the
L^{1}
-distance of
u_{n}^{\sigma,\varepsilon}
from
u
on
O^{\prime}
. We have

\displaystyle\int_{O^{\prime}}|u_{n}^{\sigma,\varepsilon}-u|\,{\rm d}x

\displaystyle\leq\int_{O^{\prime}}|u_{n}^{\sigma,\varepsilon}-v_{n}|\,{\rm d}x\ +\int_{O^{\prime}}|v_{n}-u|\,{\rm d}x\

(4.14)

\displaystyle=\sum_{\ell=1}^{\ell_{\varepsilon}}\int_{M^{\prime}_{\ell}(3\sigma)}|u_{n}^{\sigma,\varepsilon}-v_{n}|\,{\rm d}x+\int_{O^{\prime}}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}|v_{n}-u|\,{\rm d}x.

Recalling the definition of
u_{n}^{\sigma,\ell}
, we observe that

\int_{M^{\prime}_{\ell}(3\sigma)}|u_{n}^{\sigma,\varepsilon}-v_{n}|\,{\rm d}x\leq\int_{M^{\prime}_{\ell}(3\sigma)}\big(|v_{n}^{\sigma,\ell}|+|v_{n}|\big)\,{\rm d}x.

After a change of variables we obtain a constant
C_{\ell}\geq 1
such that

(4.15)

\int_{M^{\prime}_{\ell}(3\sigma)}|v_{n}^{\sigma,\ell}|\,{\rm d}x\leq C_{\ell}\int_{M^{\prime}_{\ell}(3\sigma)}|v_{n}|\,{\rm d}x,

hence,

(4.16)

\int_{M^{\prime}_{\ell}(3\sigma)}|u_{n}^{\sigma,\varepsilon}-v_{n}|\,{\rm d}x\leq 2\color[rgb]{0,0,0}C_{\ell}\int_{M^{\prime}_{\ell}(3\sigma)}|v_{n}|\,{\rm d}x\leq 2\color[rgb]{0,0,0}C_{\ell}\int_{M^{\prime}_{\ell}(3\sigma)}|v_{n}-u|\,{\rm d}x+2\color[rgb]{0,0,0}C_{\ell}\int_{M^{\prime}_{\ell}(3\sigma)}|u|\,{\rm d}x.

Since the sets
M^{\prime}_{\ell}(3\sigma)
are pairwise disjoint and contained in
O^{\prime}
and
\{v_{n}\}_{n}
converges to
u
strongly in
L^{1}(O^{\prime})
, it holds

(4.17)

\lim_{\sigma\to 0^{+}}\sum_{\ell=1}^{\ell_{\varepsilon}}\omega_{\ell}(\sigma)=0,\quad\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\text{ where }\ \ \color[rgb]{0,0,0}\omega_{\ell}(\sigma)\coloneq\lim_{n\to+\infty}\int_{M^{\prime}_{\ell}(3\sigma)}|v_{n}-u|\,{\rm d}x+\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\int_{M^{\prime}_{\ell}(3\sigma)}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}|u|\,{\rm d}x.

Finally, combining (
4.14
)–(
4.17
) and using again that
\{v_{n}\}_{n}
converges to
u
strongly in
L^{1}(O^{\prime})
,
we obtain, for every
0<\sigma<\sigma^{\varepsilon}
,

(4.18)

\limsup_{n\to+\infty}\int_{O^{\prime}}|u_{n}^{\sigma,\varepsilon}-u|\,{\rm d}x\leq 2\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\sum_{\ell=1}^{\ell_{\varepsilon}}C_{\ell}\omega_{\ell}(\sigma).\color[rgb]{0,0,0}

Step 4.
(Estimate on
\mathcal{V}
) We estimate
\mathcal{V}(u_{n}^{\sigma,\varepsilon},O^{\prime})
, given by

(4.19)

\mathcal{V}(u_{n}^{\sigma,\varepsilon},O^{\prime})=\int_{O^{\prime}}|\nabla u_{n}^{\sigma,\varepsilon}|\,{\rm d}x+\int_{O^{\prime}\cap J_{u_{n}^{\sigma,\varepsilon}}}|[u_{n}^{\sigma,\varepsilon}]|\land 1\,{\rm d}\mathcal{H}^{d-1}.

As for the first term, we have by (
4.9
) and (
4.11
)

\displaystyle\limsup_{n\to+\infty}\int_{O^{\prime}}|\nabla u_{n}^{\sigma,\varepsilon}|\,{\rm d}x

\displaystyle=\limsup_{n\to+\infty}\Big(\int_{O^{\prime}\setminus M^{\prime}(3\sigma)}|\nabla v_{n}|\,{\rm d}x+\sum_{\ell=1}^{\ell_{\varepsilon}}\int_{M^{\prime}_{\ell}(3\sigma)}|\nabla u_{n}^{\sigma,\varepsilon}|\,{\rm d}x\Big)

(4.20)

\displaystyle\leq\mathcal{V}(u,O\setminus J_{u}\color[rgb]{0,0,0})+\varepsilon\color[rgb]{0,0,0}+\limsup_{n\to+\infty}\Big(\sum_{\ell=1}^{\ell_{\varepsilon}}\int_{M^{\prime}_{\ell}(3\sigma)}|\nabla u_{n}^{\sigma,\varepsilon}|\,{\rm d}x\Big).

To estimate the last term in the previous inequality, we note that by (
4.10
) and (
4.11
) we have

\nabla u_{n}^{\sigma,\varepsilon}=\gamma_{\ell}\nabla v^{\sigma,\ell}_{n}+(1-\gamma_{\ell})\nabla v_{n}+\nabla\gamma_{\ell}\big(v_{n}^{\sigma,\ell}-v_{n}\big)\quad\text{$\mathcal{L}^{d}$-a.e.\ in $M^{\prime}_{\ell}(3\sigma)$,}

\nu_{\ell}
K_{\ell}
M_{\ell}(\sigma)\setminus M^{\prime}_{\ell}(\sigma)
M^{\prime}_{\ell}(\sigma)\setminus M^{\prime\prime}_{\ell}(\sigma)
M^{\prime\prime}_{\ell}(\sigma)
- -supp(
\gamma_{\ell}
)- -

Figure 2.
Subdivision of
M_{\ell}(\sigma)

so that

\displaystyle\int_{M^{\prime}_{\ell}(3\sigma)}|\nabla u_{n}^{\sigma,\varepsilon}|\,{\rm d}x

\displaystyle\leq\int_{M^{\prime}_{\ell}(3\sigma)}|\nabla v^{\sigma,\ell}_{n}|\,{\rm d}x+\int_{M^{\prime}_{\ell}(3\sigma)\setminus M^{\prime\prime}_{\ell}(3\sigma)}|\nabla v_{n}|\,{\rm d}x

(4.21)

\displaystyle\quad+\|\nabla\gamma_{\ell}\|_{L^{\infty}(M^{\prime}_{\ell}(3\sigma))}\int_{M^{\prime}_{\ell}(3\sigma)}|v_{n}^{\sigma,\ell}-v_{n}|\,{\rm d}x

for all
\ell\in\{1,\dots,\ell_{\varepsilon}\}
. We estimate each term separately.
For the first term, a change of variables shows that for every
\ell\in\{1,\dots,\ell_{\varepsilon}\}
there exists a constant
C_{\ell}>0
such that

(4.22)

\int_{M^{\prime}_{\ell}(3\sigma)}|\nabla v_{n}^{\sigma,\ell}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}|\,{\rm d}x\leq C_{\ell}\int_{M^{\prime}_{\ell}(3\sigma)\setminus M^{\prime}_{\ell}(\sigma)}|\nabla v_{n}|\,{\rm d}x.

We observe that
M^{\prime}_{\ell}(3\sigma)\setminus M^{\prime}_{\ell}(\sigma)\subset\subset M_{\ell}(4\sigma)\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\setminus M_{\ell}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}
. Thus, we can apply once again
[
43
, Theorem 4
′
]
, and we obtain

(4.23)

\displaystyle\limsup_{n\to+\infty}\int_{M^{\prime}_{\ell}(3\sigma)\setminus M^{\prime}_{\ell}(\sigma)}|\nabla v_{n}|\,{\rm d}x\leq\int_{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}M_{\ell}(4\sigma)\setminus M_{\ell}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}}|\nabla u|\,{\rm d}x+|D^{s}u|(\color[rgb]{0,0,0}M_{\ell}(4\sigma)\setminus M_{\ell}\color[rgb]{0,0,0})=:\lambda_{\ell}(\sigma)

for every
\ell\in\{1,\dots,\ell_{\varepsilon}\}
.
Note that
\lambda_{\ell}(\sigma)\to 0^{+}
as
\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\sigma\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\to 0^{+}
because
\color[rgb]{0,0,0}M_{\ell}(4\sigma)\setminus M_{\ell}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\searrow\mathchar 31\relax
as
\sigma\to 0^{+}
. Similarly, we can use
[
43
, Theorem 4
′
]
to estimate the second term in (
4.21
) as

(4.24)

\limsup_{n\to+\infty}\int_{M^{\prime}_{\ell}(3\sigma)\setminus M^{\prime\prime}_{\ell}(3\sigma)}|\nabla v_{n}|\,{\rm d}x\leq\int_{M_{\ell}(4\sigma)\setminus K_{\ell}}\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!|\nabla u|\,{\rm d}x+|D^{s}u|\big(M_{\ell}(4\sigma)\setminus\color[rgb]{0,0,0}K_{\ell}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\big)=:\mu_{\ell}(\sigma)

for every
\ell\in\{1,\dots,\ell_{\varepsilon}\}
. We observe that, since
M_{\ell}(4\sigma)\setminus K_{\ell}\searrow M_{\ell}\setminus K_{\ell}
, it holds

(4.25)

\limsup_{\sigma\to 0^{+}}\mu_{\ell}(\sigma)\leq\int_{M_{\ell}\setminus K_{\ell}}|[u]|\,{\rm d}\mathcal{H}^{d-1}=0,

where we used that
\mathcal{H}^{d-1}(J_{u}\cap(M_{\ell}\setminus K_{\ell}))=0
.
Finally, for the last term in (
4.21
), we use (
4.15
), the second inequality in (
4.16
), and (
4.17
) to get

(4.26)

\limsup_{n\to+\infty}\|\nabla\gamma_{\ell}\|_{L^{\infty}(M^{\prime}_{\ell}(3\sigma))}\int_{M^{\prime}_{\ell}(3\sigma)}|v_{n}^{\sigma,\ell}-v_{n}|\,{\rm d}x\leq C_{\ell}\omega_{\ell}(\sigma)

for a constant
C_{\ell}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}>0\color[rgb]{0,0,0}
depending only on
\ell
.
Combining the previous inequality with (
4.21
)–(
4.24
), we obtain, for all
\ell\in\{1,\dots,\ell_{\varepsilon}\}
,

(4.27)

\limsup_{n\to+\infty}\int_{M^{\prime}_{\ell}(3\sigma)}|\nabla u_{n}^{\sigma,\varepsilon}|\,{\rm d}x\leq C_{\ell}\lambda_{\ell}(\sigma)+\mu_{\ell}(\sigma)+C_{\ell}\omega_{\ell}(\sigma),

where
C_{\ell}>0
is (the maximum of) the constant(s) appearing in (
4.22
) and (
4.26
), respectively.

We now estimate the second term on the right-hand side of (
4.19
). To this end, we observe that by (
4.12
) and (
4.13
) we have

(4.28)

\int_{J_{u_{n}^{\sigma,\varepsilon}}\cap M_{\ell}^{\prime}}|[u_{n}^{\sigma,\varepsilon}]|\land 1\,{\rm d}\mathcal{H}^{d-1}=\int_{M^{\prime}_{\ell}}|(v_{n}(\cdot+\sigma\nu_{\ell})-v_{n}(\cdot-\sigma\nu_{\ell}))\gamma_{\ell}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}|\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\land 1\,{\rm d}\mathcal{H}^{d-1}.

Let
\partial^{\pm\sigma}_{\ell}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\coloneq M_{\ell}\pm\sigma\nu_{\ell}
. As
|[u]|\mathcal{H}^{d-1}\mathbin{\vrule height=6.88889pt,depth=0.0pt,width=0.55974pt\vrule height=0.55974pt,depth=0.0pt,width=5.59721pt}J_{u}
is a finite measure and the sets
\partial^{\pm\sigma}_{\ell}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}
are pairwise disjoint, there exists a set
S\subset(0,+\infty)
, with
(0,+\infty)\setminus S
at most countable, such that
\mathcal{H}^{d-1}(\partial^{\pm\sigma}_{\ell}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\cap J_{u})=0
for every
\sigma\in S
. By well-known properties of convolutions of
{\rm BV}
-functions, this implies that for every
\sigma\in S
the trace of
v_{n}
on
\partial^{\pm\sigma}_{\ell}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}
converges in
L^{1}_{\mathcal{H}^{d-1}}(\partial^{\pm\sigma}_{\ell}\color[rgb]{0,0,0})
to the trace of
u
on
\partial^{\pm\sigma}_{\ell}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}
